Upon the conclusion of the Proskomedia, the priest, having censed the church, standeth before the holy Altar-Table with the deacons, and having bowed reverently and signed themselves with their right hand, they say secretly: “Blessed is our God.” After this, having joined hands at their breast, they say: “Heavenly King,” etc. And signing themselves again, they say: “Glory to God in the highest,” and that twice; after this: “O Lord, open Thou my lips,” once; and they kiss the holy Altar-Table and the Cross. The deacon, after this having received a blessing from the priest, goeth forth from the holy Sanctuary by the northern door, holding the Orarion with three fingers of his hand, and standing upon the ambo directly before the holy Royal Doors, and having bowed with reverence, beginneth with a loud voice: “Bless, Master.” And the priest, having taken the holy Gospel Book into his hands, raiseth it crosswise, saying: “Blessed is the kingdom...”[^187] etc., unto which the choir answereth: “Amen.” After this the priest kisseth the Gospel Book and placeth it upon its customary place; the deacon then beginneth to chant the Litany of Peace aloud: “In peace let us pray to the Lord.” (If there is no deacon, the priest himself saith this litany.) Unto each petition the choir answereth: “Lord, have mercy.” It is to be noted here that in this litany, after the petition: “For them that travel by sea or by land...” etc., if the Liturgy is celebrated for a special intention, the appropriate prescribed litanies are added, which are found in the *Sluzhebnik* on page 642; if, however, for the departed, this one: “For the servant of God...” etc., or “For the souls of all.” (This litany for the departed, however, is not added on Sunday, nor on any feast day.) After this: “That we may be delivered...”; this Litany of Peace endeth with the exclamation: “For unto Thee is due...” etc., unto which the choir saith: “Amen.”[^188]

After the Litany of Peace the choir beginneth to sing the Psalms for the Antiphon, namely, when the 1st Antiphon (daily or festal) is sung, the priest saith the 1st prayer secretly: “O Lord our God...” etc. Upon the conclusion of this prayer he saith the Little Litany: “Again and again in peace let us pray to the Lord.”

When the choir singeth the second Antiphon, the priest saith the second prayer secretly: “O Lord our God, save Thy people...”; but when the choir beginneth to sing: “Glory to the Father... Only-begotten Son...” etc., the priest, having signed himself with the cross, with outstretched hands saith secretly likewise this hymn: “Glory to the Father... Only-begotten Son...” It is to be noted that at the word “to become incarnate” the priest boweth low. After this prayer the priest saith the Little Litany: “Again and again,” as before. — If there is a deacon, then standing before the Royal Doors during the 1st Antiphon he goeth unto the icon of the Savior; thence, when the choir beginneth to sing the 2nd Antiphon, he goeth unto the icon of the Theotokos; and at “Glory... Only-begotten...” he standeth before the Royal Doors, which the subdeacon openeth; at the word “to become incarnate” both deacon and subdeacon bow low and go unto the holy Altar-Table.

When the 3rd Antiphon beginneth to be sung, the priest, standing with the deacon and subdeacon at the holy Altar-Table, first saith the prayer: “Thou Who hast given us these common...” and after this “O Lord our God,” then maketh a bow with the deacons and taketh into his hands, or giveth unto the deacon, the holy Gospel Book and maketh the “Little Entrance,”[^189] and that thus: descending from the holy Altar-Table, he goeth behind the altar-throne to the right and goeth forth through the northern door before the iconostasis onto the ambo, candle-bearers preceding them (if there be such). And the priest holding his hands joined at the breast and having stood before the Royal Doors with the deacons, the deacon asketh a blessing from the priest, who blesseth the holy entrance with the sign of the cross, saying: “Blessed is the entrance of Thy Saints, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen,” and kisseth the holy Gospel Book; the deacon, standing again before the Royal Doors, raiseth his hands slightly with the holy Gospel Book crosswise and exclaimeth: “Wisdom! Stand upright!”[^190] Unto this the choir singeth: “Come, let us worship...” etc.; the priest with the deacons entereth into the holy Sanctuary [Altar], and the deacon, having bowed, placeth the holy Gospel Book upon the holy Altar-Table.

After this, the troparia and kontakia are sung according to custom; the priest, standing before the holy Altar-Table, readeth the prayer of the Trisagion hymn, having his hands joined at his breast: “O holy God, Who restest in the saints...” etc. Having concluded this prayer, he exclaimeth: “For holy art Thou, our God, and unto Thee do we send up glory, to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit, always, now and forever.” At the words “Now and forever” the priest turneth toward the people, blessing them, and the deacon concludeth the exclamation, saying: “And unto the ages of ages.” Unto this the choir, having sung “Amen,” beginneth to sing the “Trisagion.” The priest with the deacons say also themselves the “Trisagion,” making together three bows before the holy Altar-Table. At “Glory to the Father” they leave the holy Altar-Table and for the last time say the “Trisagion” already at the foot of the holy Altar-Table. After this they depart unto the High Place, but first the deacon and subdeacon change places, passing between the priest and the holy Altar-Table, and in that manner the deacon will be on the left, and the subdeacon on the right side. The departing priest on the right side departing unto the High Place quietly saith the prayer: “Blessed is He That cometh in the name of the Lord; blessed art Thou upon the throne of the glory of Thy kingdom, Who sittest upon the Cherubim, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages.”

Upon the conclusion of the “Trisagion” the deacon exclaimeth: “Let us attend”; the priest: “Peace be unto all”; and the subdeacon: “Wisdom! Let us attend!”[^191] After this the Prokimenon of the Sunday, of the Feast, or of various intentions is sung. After the Prokimenon the deacon exclaimeth: “Wisdom!” and the subdeacon or reader saith the title of the holy Apostle (“The Reading from the Acts of the Holy Apostles,” or “The Reading from the Catholic Epistle of Peter,” or “The Reading from the Epistle of the Holy Apostle Paul to the Romans”), whereunto the deacon exclaimeth: “Let us attend!” and the subdeacon or reader beginneth to read the holy Epistle, introducing it with either: “Brethren,” or: “In those days,” or: “Child Timothy,” which are appointed in the Apostle book for the apostrophe [address].[^192]

During the reading of the Epistle, the deacon, having taken the censer and asked a blessing from the priest, censeth the holy Altar-Table and the holy Prothesis from the four sides. After the Epistle hath been read, the priest saith: “Peace be unto thee”; and the deacon, standing at the Royal Doors turned toward the people whom he censeth, addeth: “Wisdom! Let us attend!” (If there is no deacon, the priest himself saith it.) The choir unto this answereth: “Alleluia,”[^193] during the singing of which the priest quietly saith the prayer: “Shine forth in our hearts, O Master Who lovest mankind, the undefiled light of Thy divine knowledge, and open the eyes of our mind to the understanding of Thy gospel proclamations; implant in us also the fear of Thy blessed commandments, that trampling down all carnal desires, we may enter upon a spiritual manner of living, both thinking and doing all things that are well-pleasing unto Thee. For Thou art the enlightenment of our souls and bodies, O Christ God, and unto Thee do we send up glory, together with Thine unoriginate Father, and Thy Most Holy, good, and life-creating Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen.” The deacon or the priest then handeth over the censer, and goeth unto the holy Altar-Table.

After this, both deacons stand by the holy Altar-Table, and upon the conclusion of the “Alleluia” the senior deacon taketh the Gospel Book into his hands and having set it upright upon the holy Altar-Table, exclaimeth: “Bless, Master, the good tidings of the holy, glorious, and all-praised Apostle and Evangelist, [N.]”; and the priest, standing at the High Place, blesseth the deacon, saying with a loud voice: “May God, through the prayers of the holy, glorious, and all-praised Apostle and Evangelist, [N.], give unto thee a word with great power unto the proclamation of the Gospel of His beloved Son, our Lord Jesus Christ.”

While the priest saith this, the deacon goeth forth from the holy Sanctuary, namely: the senior deacon holding the Gospel Book in his hands through the door on the right side, and the subdeacon through the door on the left side; and having come onto the ambo at the Royal Doors, they bow and, preceded by candle-bearers, go to the left before the icon of Saint Nicholas, and here the subdeacon exclaimeth: “Wisdom! Stand upright! Let us hear the Holy Gospel”; and the deacon continueth thus: “The Reading from the Holy Gospel according to [N.],” unto which the choir answereth: “Glory be to Thee, O Lord.” After this the subdeacon exclaimeth: “Let us attend!” and the deacon, with the words: “At that time,” or “The Lord said,” or “The Lord said unto the Jews that came unto Him,” or “The Lord spake this parable,” beginneth to read the Gospel. After the Gospel hath been read, the choir singeth: “Glory be to Thee, O Lord, glory be to Thee!” After this the subdeacon holding the Gospel Book in his hands, with the deacon preceded by candle-bearers, goeth upon the ambo unto the Royal Doors, and here the subdeacon handeth the Gospel Book opened unto the priest, who during this time cometh hither from the High Place, and having kissed the Gospel Book, taketh and carrieth it unto the holy Altar-Table. (If, however, the priest celebrateth alone, he himself readeth the Holy Gospel at the Royal Doors, signing himself with the cross at the beginning of the reading at “let us attend,” looking toward the people.)

Upon the conclusion of the Gospel the subdeacon goeth unto the holy Altar-Table to the priest, and the deacon, standing again in the customary place before the Royal Doors, beginneth the Insistent Litany: “Let us all say,” etc.; while the priest, standing before the holy Altar-Table, saith secretly this litany with hands joined at his breast. It is to be noted that in this litany the priest may add also a special litany, if he hath a special intention.[^194] At the exclamation of the priest: “For a merciful...” the deacon entereth by the right door, while the subdeacon goeth forth by the left door and standeth in the customary place, and beginneth the 2nd litany, which is called “For the Catechumens.”

Unto this litany the subdeacon summoneth the faithful to prayer for the Catechumens, saying: “Pray unto the Lord, ye catechumens,” etc. (The priest saith this litany secretly.) When the subdeacon cometh unto the words: “That He may reveal unto them the Gospel of righteousness,” then the priest, having kissed the Gospel Book, placeth it upon the right side. After the exclamation: “That they also with us may glorify Thine all-honorable and majestic name...” etc., the senior deacon—standing beside the priest—turneth toward the people and exclaimeth: “All ye who are catechumens, depart”; and the subdeacon—standing upon the ambo—continueth: “Ye catechumens, depart”; and thereafter the senior deacon saith again: “All ye who are catechumens, depart”; and the subdeacon continueth: “Let none of the catechumens remain...” etc., and hereby the Liturgy of the Catechumens is concluded. And after this beginneth the III. Part of the Liturgy: The Liturgy of the Faithful.

§. 141.

(c) The Order of the Liturgy of the Faithful.

When the catechumens have been summoned by the deacon’s exclamation to depart the church, the priest unfoldeth upon the holy Altar-Table the folded Iliton, as it were for a sign that before the eyes of the faithful the greatest mystery instituted by Jesus Christ for our salvation is unveiled and accomplished. And after this the priest readeth the 1st Prayer of the Faithful, saying secretly: “We give thanks unto Thee, O Lord God of Hosts...” etc. And while the priest readeth the prayer, the deacon exclaimeth: “Help us, save us, have mercy on us...” etc., unto which the choir answereth: “Lord, have mercy.” And immediately before the exclamation the deacon saith: “Wisdom!” and the priest with a loud voice pronounceth the exclamation: “For unto Thee is due all glory, honor, and worship...” etc. After this exclamation the deacon saith: “Again and again in peace let us pray to the Lord,” and the priest readeth the 2nd Prayer of the Faithful. During the prayer of the priest the subdeacon saith again: “Help us, save us, have mercy on us, and protect us by Thy grace,” unto which the choir singeth: “Lord, have mercy.” Before the exclamation: “That being ever guarded under Thy dominion...” etc., the subdeacon saith again: “Wisdom!”[^195] and the priest maketh the exclamation, in which at the words: “now and forever” he turneth toward the people, blessing them.

After this the subdeacon entereth into the holy Sanctuary [Altar], and having opened the Royal Doors, goeth unto the priest; the senior deacon, having received the cen- -ser and a blessing from the priest, descendeth from the holy Altar-Table, censeth the holy Altar-Table round about, likewise also the Table of Preparation [Prothesis], and coming unto the Royal Doors censeth thence first the icon of the Savior, thereafter that of the Theotokos, and finally the people. After this he entereth unto the Altar-Table, bringing the censer with him. (If there is no deacon, the priest himself censeth, reciting Psalm 50.) After this the priest, standing at the holy Altar-Table, saith first the prayer: “None is worthy of them that are bound with carnal lusts and pleasures...” etc., and after this Psalm 50. At the last the priest, having taken the censer into his left hand from the deacon, with outstretched hands saith thrice, signing himself with the cross: “Let us who mystically represent the Cherubim, and sing the thrice-holy hymn unto the life-creating Trinity, now lay aside all earthly care...” etc. After each repetition he maketh a bow, and after the third repetition, having bowed, he kisseth the holy Altar-Table and the Cross. After this he goeth forth unto the Royal Doors, and having censed the holy Sanctuary [Altar], he departeth unto the Table of Preparation [Prothesis], the deacons preceding him, where he likewise censeth the offered gifts, praying within himself: “O God, be merciful unto me a sinner!” (While the priest prayeth and the deacon censeth, the choir singeth: “Let us who mystically represent the Cherubim...”[^196])

Here at the Table of Preparation [Prothesis] the priest, having censed the gifts, giveth the censer unto the subdeacon; and having taken the Aer, giveth it likewise unto the subdeacon, who, having received it into both hands, holdeth it before his face and carrieth it, candle-bearers preceding them. Having taken the Diskos with the Asterisk [Star], he giveth it unto the deacon, who, upon the conclusion of the singing of “Let us who mystically represent the Cherubim,” exclaimeth: “May the Lord God remember all you Orthodox Christians in His kingdom, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages.” (If the deacon is only a cleric, he then carrieth the censer.) The priest after this, having taken the holy Chalice into his hands, goeth forth after the deacons through the northern door. (If, however, the priest celebrateth alone, then, having placed the Aer upon his left shoulder and having given up the censer, he taketh into his right hand the Diskos, upon which is the Asterisk and one veil, and into his left hand the Chalice covered with the lention and veil), chanting: “The Most Holy Universal Pontiff, [N.], our most faithful Sovereign, [N.], with all his court and his Christ-loving armed forces, our God-loving Bishop, [N.], and all the priestly and diaconal order, and the clerical rank; the blessed and ever-memorable founders and benefactors of this holy temple; and may the Lord God remember all you Orthodox Christians in His kingdom, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages.” And having come before the Royal Doors, at the words: “and all you...” etc., he turneth toward the people, but blesseth them not; after this he turneth again unto the holy Altar-Table and thus concludeth this prayer. Meanwhile the deacon and subdeacon, having entered into the holy Sanctuary [Altar], await the priest on both sides within the holy doors. At the conclusion of this Great Entrance the choir saith “Amen,” and singeth: “That we may receive the King of all, escorted invisibly by angelic ranks.*) Alleluia.”[^197],[^198]

Having entered***)[^199] within into the holy Sanctuary [Altar], the priest setteth the holy Chalice upon the holy Altar-Table upon the Iliton on the right side, having removed from it the lention and having covered it with the smaller veil; and upon the left side he placeth the Diskos, having the Asterisk [Star] standing over the Lamb. Then, taking the Aer and having censed it, he covereth therewith the presented gifts, saying: “The noble Joseph, taking down Thy most pure Body from the tree, wrapped it in clean linen with sweet spices, and laying it in a new tomb, closed it...” etc. After this, taking the censer from the deacon, he censeth them thrice, saying: “Do good, O Lord, in Thy good pleasure unto Zion, and let the walls of Jerusalem be built...” etc.

The priest having thus placed the Holy Gifts, the deacon boweth low before the most holy Altar-Table and goeth forth through the northern door onto the ambo, and standing in the customary place, saith: “Let us complete our prayer unto the Lord,” etc.; while the priest quietly saith this litany at the holy Altar-Table, and concludeth with the exclamation: “Through the compassions of Thine Only-begotten Son, with Whom Thou art blessed, together with Thine all-holy, good, and life-creating Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages,” unto which the choir answereth: “Amen.”

When the Holy Gifts have been offered unto God in sacrifice, and when now the unbloody sacrifice of the New Testament itself is to be accomplished: the faithful are bound to prepare themselves for the accomplishment of this great Mystery, that this sacrifice may be pleasing unto God also with regard to the faithful. But since without faith it is impossible to please God, and faith without love is dead: therefore the faithful must manifest their love before the oblation of the sacrifice, and unanimously confess their faith.

Unto the sign of that mutual love, the priest expresseth it when after the exclamation: “Through the compassions” he saith unto the faithful the words: “Peace be unto all.” Unto this the choir answereth: “And unto thy spirit.” After this the priest or deacon summoneth the faithful to manifest also by an outward sign their love, saying: “Let us love one another, that with one mind we may confess:”[^200] Unto this the choir answereth: “The Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit: the Trinity, one in essence and undivided.” During this singing the priest kisseth the Gifts covered with the Aer: first the top of the Diskos, then the top of the Chalice, thereafter the edge of the holy Altar-Table before him, saying at each kissing: “I will love Thee, O Lord, my strength; the Lord is my foundation and my refuge.”

After this kissing the priest or deacon exclaimeth: “The doors! The doors! In wisdom let us attend!”[^201] unto which the choir singeth: “I believe.” And the priest, having raised the Aer with both hands, holdeth it over the Holy Gifts, reciting secretly the Confession of Faith. (If there is a deacon, he during this time openeth the Royal Doors.) When the priest cometh unto the words: “and was incarnate of the Holy Spirit and Mary the Virgin,” he boweth low; and upon the conclusion of the Symbol [Creed] he lowereth the Aer, and having folded it in three parts, saith the Trisagion, and signing himself with the sign of the cross, kisseth the Aer, and having folded it, placeth it upon the right side of the holy Altar-Table.

After the Confession of Faith, in order to make the faithful attentive unto the unbloody sacrifice, the deacon or priest exclaimeth: “Let us stand aright, let us stand with fear, let us attend, that we may offer the holy oblation in peace,” and immediately the deacon, having bowed, entereth into the holy Sanctuary [Altar], and the priest exclaimeth, blessing the people: “The grace of our Lord Jesus Christ, and the love of God the Father, and the communion of the Holy Spirit, be with you all,” unto which the choir answereth: “And with thy spirit.” After this the priest again admonisheth the faithful to lay aside all earthly cares, saying: “Let us lift up our hearts,” lifting up together both his hands and his eyes, unto which the choir answereth: “We lift them up unto the Lord.” After this the priest, having joined his outstretched hands at his breast and bowed his head, saith: “Let us give thanks unto the Lord,” and while the choir singeth: “It is proper and right...” etc., he, with outstretched hands, reverently readeth the prayer: “It is proper and right...” and pronounceth only the conclusion of this prayer with a loud voice: “Singing the triumphant hymn, shouting, crying aloud, and saying:”[^202] and at these words taketh the Asterisk [Star], and having bowed, striketh the four edges of the star upon the four sides of the Diskos, that is, depicteth therewith upon the Diskos the image of the Holy Cross for a sign that the remembrance of Christ’s death upon the Cross draweth near, and kisseth it, and setteth it aside.

When the choir singeth the Angelic hymn, “Holy, Holy, Holy,” the priest—since he himself beforehand, having joined his hands at his breast, hath recited the hymn “Holy, Holy, Holy”—must renew with discernment and attentiveness the intention of his will toward the consecration of the Holy Mysteries, which he already formed at the Proskomedia, and that: briefly, quietly, in mind and not by words, saying: that he desireth to consecrate the bread into the Body and the wine into the Blood; likewise he quietly repeateth within himself for whom, or for what necessity, he desireth to offer this sacrifice. And having awakened within himself perfect contrition and gathered all his thoughts, he outstretcheth his hands and with God-fearing attentiveness readeth secretly this prayer: “With these blessed powers we also, O Master Who lovest mankind, cry aloud and say: Holy art Thou and all-holy, Thou and Thine Only-begotten Son, and Thy Holy Spirit...” etc.; and when he cometh in this prayer unto the words: “taking bread,” he joineth his hands; and after this taketh the Diskos into his hands and lifteth it up; and saying: “having given thanks,” he inclineth his head, and maketh with his right hand over the Diskos the sign of the cross, saying: “He † blessed, sanc † tified, and † broke, and gave unto His disciples, saying:” And immediately having bowed down, looking attentively upon the prepared bread, he pronounceth the words that consecrate and transubstantiate the bread into the Body of Christ with a loud voice and distinctly: “Take, eat: this is My Body, which is broken for you for the remission of sins.” The choir: “Amen.” After this, having bowed low once, he uncovereth the holy Chalice and, taking it with both hands, blesseth it quietly, saying: “Likewise also the cup, † after supper, saying:” and immediately having bowed down and looking into the Chalice, he pronounceth with a loud voice the words that consecrate and transubstantiate the wine into the Blood of Jesus Christ: “Drink of it, all of you: this is My Blood of the New Testament, which is shed for you and for many for the remission of sins.” When the choir singeth: “Amen,” the priest again boweth low before the holy Blood of Christ once, and after this quietly saith: “Remembering therefore this saving commandment and all those things which came to pass for us: the Cross, the Grave, the Resurrection on the third day, the Ascension into heaven, the Sitting at the right hand, and the second and glorious Coming again...” etc. Upon the conclusion whereof, having crossed his hands crosswise, he then taketh the Diskos into his right hand, and the Chalice into his left, and raiseth them up from the holy Altar-Table and setteth them down upon the holy Altar-Table crosswise, saying: “Thine own of Thine own we offer unto Thee, in all and for all,” unto which the choir singeth: “We sing unto Thee, we bless Thee, we give thanks unto Thee, O Lord, and we pray unto Thee, our God.”[^203]

After this the priest, having placed the Holy Mysteries in their place, boweth low, and with outstretched hands secretly readeth the prayer: “Furthermore we offer unto Thee this reasonable and unbloody service...” etc. Upon the conclusion of this prayer he saith thrice in a quiet voice: “O Lord, Who didst send down Thy Most Holy Spirit at the third hour upon Thine Apostles: take Him not away from us, O Good One, but renew Him in us who pray unto Thee,” with the verses, and after each time boweth low; and at the end blesseth the consecrated bread, saying: “And make this bread † the precious Body of Thy Christ”; and after this blesseth the consecrated wine, saying: “And that which is in this cup, † the precious Blood of Thy Christ”; and after this, signing both with the cross, saith: “Changing them † by Thy Holy Spirit.” And having bowed profoundly, he covereth the Chalice and continueth the initial prayer: “That they may be unto them that partake for the purification of soul, for the remission of sins, for the communion of Thy Holy Spirit, for the fulfillment of the kingdom of heaven...” etc.[^204]

After this the priest, having commended all the faithful unto God’s grace, commemorateth the individual members of the Church of Christ, and that first of all the members of the Church Triumphant (*Ecclesia triumphans*), with outstretched hands quietly saying: “Furthermore we offer unto Thee this reasonable service for those who have fallen asleep in the faith: Ancestors, Fathers, Patriarchs, Prophets, Apostles, Preachers, Evangelists, Martyrs, Confessors, Ascetics, and every righteous spirit made perfect in faith...” etc. But in this prayer, when he cometh unto the commemoration of the Most Holy Theotokos, he taketh the censer and censeth the Holy Mysteries, continuing the initial prayer, saying with a loud voice: “Especially for our Most Holy, most pure, most blessed and glorious Lady, the Theotokos and Ever-Virgin Mary,” unto which the choir singeth: “It is truly proper to bless thee, the Theotokos, ever-blessed and blameless, and the Mother of our God...” After this the priest outstretcheth his hands and continueth the commemoration of the Saints, quietly saying: “For the holy Prophet, Forerunner, and Baptist John,” etc. If there is a deacon, at “Especially” he openeth the Royal Doors and censeth the holy Altar-Table crosswise.

After this the priest, having invoked the Saints for their intercession, commemorateth all the departed in the hope of resurrection (*Ecclesia patiens*), saying: “Remember, O Lord, the soul of Thy servant, [N.]...” etc., whereat he joineth his hands, and quietly commemorateth their names, and at the end outstretcheth his hands and concludeth the commemoration of the departed with the words: “And make them to rest where the light of Thy countenance visiteth.”

After the commemoration of the dead followeth the commemoration of the living (*Ecclesia militans*), quietly and with outstretched hands: “Furthermore we entreat Thee, remember, O Lord, all the episcopate of the Orthodox...” etc. Nevertheless, the commemoration of the supreme ecclesiastical hierarchs is made with a loud voice by this exclamation: “Among the first, remember, O Lord, the Most Holy Universal Pontiff, [N.], and our God-loving Bishop, [N.],” etc. After this exclamation, while the choir singeth: “And all men and all women,” the priest, having his hands joined, continueth the commemoration of the living whom he wisheth, having commemorated also himself. At the end he outstretcheth his hands and quietly continueth the commemorations, saying: “Remember, O Lord, this city wherein we dwell...” etc., and concludeth with the exclamation: “And grant that with one mouth and one heart we may glorify and praise Thine all-honorable and majestic name, of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages,”[^205] etc.

Having finished all these commemorations, he entreateth God that the mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ, which flowed forth from His death upon the Cross as fruit, may be always with us, saying: “And may the mercies of our great God and Savior Jesus Christ be with you all.”

And hereby the core of the Divine Liturgy[^206] is concluded, that is, the oblation of the sacrifice, and now followeth the preparation for Holy Communion.

At the exclamation: “And may the mercies...” the deacon closeth the Royal Doors, and the subdeacon goeth forth before the Royal Doors onto the ambo and chanteth the litany: “Having commemorated all the Saints, again and again in peace let us pray to the Lord...” etc., unto which the choir at each petition answereth accordingly either “Lord, have mercy” or “Grant this, O Lord.” The priest, having concluded the commemorations, quietly saith the litany and the prayer: “Unto Thee we commend our whole life and hope, O Master Who lovest mankind...” etc., and when the deacon concludeth the litany, [the priest] with a loud voice pronounceth the exclamation: “And make us worthy, O Master, that with boldness and without condemnation we may dare to call upon Thee, the heavenly God as Father, and to say:” etc., unto which the choir singeth “Our Father.” During the singing thereof the priest, having his hands outstretched, likewise saith the Lord’s Prayer, but secretly—pronouncing with a loud voice only the exclamation: “For Thine is the kingdom, and the power, and the glory, of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages.” After this the priest greeteth the faithful with these words: “Peace be unto all.” Further he summoneth them, that in token of submission unto the Heavenly Father they should bow their heads, saying: “Bow your heads unto the Lord.” During the singing of “Unto Thee, O Lord,” the priest saith the prayer: “We give thanks unto Thee, O King invisible, Who by Thine immeasurable power hast created all things, and in the multitude of Thy mercy hast brought all things from nothing into being...” etc., and concludeth with the exclamation, saying: “Through the grace and compassions and love for mankind of Thine Only-begotten Son, with Whom Thou art blessed, together with Thine all-holy, good, and life-creating Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen.”

After this the priest, having bowed low before the Holy Mysteries, steppeth aside a little unto the right side, and washeth the fingers of both hands, wherewith he is to touch the Most Holy Body of the Lord—and having wiped them, cometh again unto the midst of the holy Altar-Table and boweth low before the Holy Mysteries.

Having washed his hands and lifted up his eyes on high, he prayeth attentively and secretly: “Hear us, O Lord Jesus Christ our God, from Thy holy dwelling-place and from the throne of the glory of Thy kingdom...” etc., upon the conclusion of which prayer he saith thrice (striking his breast): “O God, be merciful unto me a sinner!”

The deacon (if there is one), while the priest saith the prayer: “Hear us, O Lord...” etc., cometh from the ambo into the holy Sanctuary [Altar] and girdeth his orarion crosswise in order to manifest his reverence for the Holy Gifts in imitation of the Seraphim, who with wings cover their faces during the sacred action—and goeth forth again before the Royal Doors onto the ambo. When he seeth that the priest hath uncovered the holy Chalice, he boweth low and exclaimeth: “Let us attend!” and having entered into the holy Sanctuary [Altar], closeth the Royal Doors. And the priest, having taken into his hands the Holy Lamb, elevating it over the Diskos, exclaimeth with a loud voice: “Holy things are for the holy!”[^207] unto which the choir singeth: “One is holy, one is Lord, Jesus Christ, to the glory of God the Father. Amen”; and the priest, having chanted “Holy things are for the holy,” boweth low and breaketh the Holy Lamb into four parts over the Diskos, saying: “Broken and divided is the Lamb of God, Which is broken and not divided, Which is ever eaten and never consumed, but sanctifieth them that partake,” and placeth them upon the Diskos crosswise in token of the crucifixion of Christ upon the Cross—and that in such manner that he placeth the first part of the Holy Lamb with the letters “IC” above, “XC” below, “NI” on the left side, and “KA” on the right side. After this, having bowed low, he taketh the first particle (that is, “IC”) with the first two fingers of both hands, moveth it over the Chalice, and maketh therewith the sign of the Holy Cross, and with the prayer: “The fullness of the Holy Spirit,”*) placeth it into the Chalice.[^208] And having covered the holy Chalice, he boweth low. And immediately the fingers wherewith he broke the Holy Lamb he joineth together, and until the washing of the holy Chalice holdeth them thus joined.

After this, having slightly inclined his head, he outstretcheth his hands and, looking reverently upon the Holy Mysteries, quietly prayeth: “I believe, O Lord, and I confess that Thou art truly the Christ, the Son of the living God, Who camest into the world to save sinners, of whom I am the first...” etc. Upon the conclusion of this prayer he boweth low and, having taken the particle of the Lamb with the fingers of his right hand, saith: “The precious, most holy, and most pure Body of our Lord and God and Savior Jesus Christ is imparted unto me, [N.], priest, for the remission of my sins and unto life everlasting. Amen,” and having bowed down, receiveth communion over the Diskos with the particle “XC” of the Body of Christ with fear and attentiveness. (The remaining particles remain for those who receive communion.) After this, having joined his hands, he meditateth a little upon the Holy Mysteries. And having uncovered the holy Chalice, he boweth low and, taking the Diskos into his left hand, sweepeth the remaining particles of the Holy Body carefully with the thumb of his right hand into the Chalice—and covereth the Chalice with the lention. Then he taketh the Chalice into his left hand, and the Diskos into his right hand, and placeth the Diskos under the Chalice and saith: “The precious, most holy, and most pure Blood of our Lord and God and Savior Jesus Christ is imparted unto me, [N.], priest, for the remission of my sins and unto life everlasting. Amen,” and receiveth communion of the Most Holy Blood once only, that is, he drinketh of the Most Holy Blood only once, and with the lention wipeth his lips. Having thus received communion, he setteth the Chalice again upon the Iliton, and having joined his hands, kisseth the edge of the holy Chalice, saying: “Behold, this hath touched my lips, and shall take away mine iniquities and cleanse my sins.” Afterwards he covereth the Chalice with the lention and the veil, and boweth low.[^209] While the communion of the priest taketh place, the choir singeth the Communion Hymn,***) of the day, or of the Sunday, or of the feast.[^210]

After this the priest taketh the holy Chalice into his hands and turneth toward the peo- -ple and exclaimeth:*) “With the fear of God and with faith draw near,”[^211] unto which the choir singeth: “Blessed is He That cometh in the name of the Lord; the Lord is God, and hath appeared unto us.”

(Here followeth the communion of the laity, of which mention will be made in the III. Part.)

After “Blessed is He That cometh,” the priest, holding in both hands the holy Chalice covered with the lention and veil, blesseth the people with the Chalice, saying: “O God, save Thy people, and bless Thine inheritance.” Unto which the choir, confessing the benefits of the Savior, rendereth thanksgiving unto the Holy Trinity with the following hymn: “For many years, O Master. We have seen the true light, we have received the heavenly Spirit, we have found the true faith, worshiping the undivided Trinity: for the same hath saved us.” The priest at that time turneth by that same side (that is, to the right) unto the holy Altar-Table, setteth the holy Chalice upon the Iliton, and having bowed low thrice, saith: “Be Thou exalted above the heavens, O God, and Thy glory over all the earth,” and immediately taketh the Chalice with both hands—and having placed all the veils and the Diskos and the Asterisk [Star] beneath the Chalice, maketh the sign of the cross over the Altar-Table, saying: “Blessed is our God,” and turneth toward the people and blesseth the people with the holy Chalice, continuing with a loud voice the initial prayer: “always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages.”[^212]

And immediately the priest carrieth the Holy Gifts unto the Table of Preparation [Prothesis],***)[^213] and the choir, having chanted “Amen,” glorifieth and thanketh the Lord with a hymn of praise: “May our mouths be filled with Thy praise, O Lord, that we may sing of Thy glory: for Thou hast made us worthy to partake of Thy holy, divine, immortal, and life-creating Mysteries...” etc. At the Table of Preparation [Prothesis] the priest placeth the Chalice in the midst, but the Aer with one veil, and the Diskos with the Asterisk [Star] on his left side, and uncovereth the holy Chalice and boweth low, and after this beginneth to consume the Holy Mysteries, but so that he placeth the Diskos—as before in the drinking—beneath the Chalice. After this, with wine which the deacon or server poureth, he rinseth the Chalice and drinketh the purification; at the last, with wine and water he washeth the Chalice and the fingers of both his hands, and having wiped them with the lention, drinketh from the Chalice the wine and water remaining in the holy Chalice, that is, the ablution. And having wiped his mouth and the Chalice with the same lention, he foldeth the vessels, covereth them with the veils and the Aer, saying within himself: “May our mouths be filled...” etc., and after this the litany: “Having received the divine, holy, immaculate, immortal, heavenly, and life-creating, awesome Mysteries of Christ, let us worthily give thanks unto the Lord...” etc.

Upon the conclusion of the litany, the priest, having taken the holy Gospel Book from the right side, maketh therewith the sign of the holy cross over the Iliton, saying the exclamation: “For Thou art our sanctification, and unto Thee we ascribe glory: to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages.” The choir singeth: “Amen.” The priest: “Let us go forth in peace.” The choir: “In the name of the Lord.” The deacon: “Let us pray to the Lord.” The choir: “Lord, have mercy.” After this the priest goeth forth through the Royal Doors, and standing in the midst of the church (below the ambo)[^214] saith the prayer (the Prayer Behind the Ambo): “O Lord, Who blessest them that bless Thee, and sanctifiest them that put their trust in Thee...”[^215] etc. Unto such prayer the choir singeth: “Amen.” After this: “Blessed be the name of the Lord from this time forth, and unto the ages” (thrice), and Psalm 33: “I will bless the Lord at all times.” During the singing of this psalm the priest distributeth unto the faithful the antidoron[^216] (blessed bread), in memory of that which the Christians of the first centuries left the holy bread unto the poor. After the distribution of the antidoron the priest entereth through the Royal Doors into the holy Sanctuary [Altar], and standing before the holy Altar-Table quietly saith the prayer: “Thou Who art Thyself the fulfillment of the Law and the Prophets, O Christ our God...” etc. After this: “Let us pray to the Lord.” The choir: “Lord, have mercy.” The priest: “The blessing of the Lord be upon you, through His grace and love for mankind, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages.” The choir: “Amen.” The priest turneth unto the holy Altar-Table and saith: “Glory to Thee, O Christ God, our hope, glory to Thee!” The choir: “Glory be to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen. Lord, have mercy (thrice). Bless.” After this the priest turneth toward the people and pronounceth the dismissal: “May Christ our true God, through the prayers of His most pure Mother...” etc. After the dismissal the choir singeth: “Many years.” And the priest, having bowed thrice before the holy Altar-Table, saith the prayers of thanksgiving: “Glory to Thee, O God; glory to Thee, O God; glory to Thee, O God,” and the prayer of St. Simeon: “Now lettest Thou Thy servant depart in peace, O Master...” etc., the Trisagion through the Our Father, the Troparion of St. John Chrysostom: “The grace of thy mouth shining forth...” etc., the Kontakion: “From heaven hast thou received divine grace...” etc., “Lord, have mercy” (12 times), “More honorable than the Cherubim,” “Glory be to the Father... now and forever,” and maketh the small dismissal. After this he divesteth himself of the sacred vestments, saying: “Now lettest Thou Thy servant depart,” and kisseth the holy Altar-Table.

Note 1. The hymn: “Only-begotten Son”[^217] is customarily sung at the Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom and St. Basil the Great after the second litany. But if this Liturgy is served on a Saturday, and a Feast of the Mother of God should occur, then instead of “Only-begotten Son” the Dogmatikon of the Feast of the Mother of God is sung.

Note 2. If several priests concelebrate, then at the Great Entrance the serving priest saith: “The Most Holy Universal... our God-loving Bishop, and all the priestly order...”[^218] And all the concelebrants say: “May the Lord God remember thy priesthood in His kingdom, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages.”[^219]

#### ARTICLE II. Concerning the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great.

§. 142. The Order of the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great without Vespers.

The Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great is celebrated 10 times in the year: 1. On the day of Saint Basil the Great (January 1); 2. On the first five Sundays of Great Lent; 3. On Great Thursday; 4. On Great Saturday; 5. On the Eve of the Nativity of Christ; 6. On the Eve of the Theophany of the Lord.

The order of this Liturgy is entirely the same as that of the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom, with this distinction only: 1. that the priestly prayers in the Liturgy of Saint Basil are longer, on which account the singers sing the chants more slowly and at greater length, that the priest during that time may quietly read his prayers; 2. the priest at the words of consecration doth not say: “Take, eat,” but first saith: “He gave it unto His holy disciples and Apostles, saying: Take, eat: this is My Body...” Likewise also over the holy chalice he first saith: “He gave it unto His holy disciples and Apostles, saying: Drink of it, all of you: this is My Blood...”[^220] 3. Instead of “It is truly right” there is sung: “All of creation rejoiceth in thee, O full of grace, the assembly of Angels and the human race: sanctified temple and spiritual paradise, virgin boast, from whom God was incarnate and became a child, Who is our God before the ages; for He made thy womb a throne, and shaped thy body more spacious than the heavens. All of creation rejoiceth in thee, O full of grace, glory to thee!”[^221] 4. The Prayer Behind the Ambo is different: “O Master rich in mercy...”

§. 143. The Order of the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great with Vespers.

With Vespers the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great is celebrated: 1. On the Eve of the Nativity of Christ; 2. On the Eve of the Theophany; 3. On Great Thursday; 4. On Great Saturday. If the Eve of the Nativity of Christ or of the Theophany should fall on a Saturday or Sunday, then on the Eve the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom is celebrated, and on the feast day itself that of Saint Basil the Great.

The order of this Liturgy with Vespers is as follows:

After the initial exclamation: “Blessed is the kingdom...” there is read: “Come, let us worship,” and the Introductory Psalm 103: “Bless the Lord, O my soul.” The deacon readeth the prayers of the Lucernarium. After the psalm there is the Litany of Peace. After the litany: “Lord, I have cried” on 8, at the end the Theotokion or Dogmatikon, during which the entrance is made with the censer (and if the Gospel Book is to be read, then with the Gospel Book). After the entrance: “O Joyful Light.” After this the deacon: “Let us attend!” The priest: “Peace be unto all.” The deacon: “Wisdom! Let us attend!” and the Prokimenon of the day. After the Prokimenon the Paremias are read (on the Eve of the Nativity 8, of the Theophany 13, on Great Thursday 3, on Great Saturday 15). After the Paremias there is the small litany, and the Trisagion (but on Great Thursday and Great Saturday, instead of the Trisagion: “All ye who have been baptized into Christ”). After the Trisagion the Prokimenon, Epistle, Alleluia, and Gospel. After the Gospel, the customary order of the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great.

Note. The Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom is likewise celebrated with Vespers on the day of the Annunciation,[^222] if it falleth upon any weekday of Great Lent, except Saturday and Sunday.

#### ARTICLE III. The Order of the Divine Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts.

The Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts is a divine service wherein the unbloody sacrifice is not accomplished, that is, there is no consecration of the gifts (bread and wine), but communion taketh place with the Gifts presanctified at a full Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom or Saint Basil the Great. A full Liturgy is not served[^223] on all days of Great Lent, except Saturdays and Sundays, and the Feast of the Annunciation; but that the faithful might not be deprived of Holy Communion during this holy time of repentance, it was enacted by the Synod of Laodicea[^224] and the Synod in Trullo to celebrate on Wednesdays and Fridays of Great Lent the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts.

The order of this Liturgy consisteth of 4 parts: (a) The Preparation of the Holy Lamb, (b) Vespers, (c) The Liturgy of the Catechumens, (d) The Liturgy of the Faithful.

§. 144. (a) The Preparation of the Holy Lamb.

The Holy Lamb[^225] for the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts is prepared at a full Liturgy on the preceding Sunday (or Saturday), when there is prepared not one Lamb alone, but two or three (according to the number of Presanctified Liturgies to be celebrated). At the Proskomedia over each Lamb the priest doth the same as over the first Lamb. At the consecration of the gifts, however, the priest pronounceth the words of consecration once only in the plural number: “Make these breads the precious Body of Thy Christ...” And at the fraction of the Lamb the priest taketh the first Lamb and inciseth it and placeth a particle into the chalice; the other Lambs he doth not divide, but having dipped the spoon into the Holy Blood, he anointeth them crosswise on the side of the incision with the Holy Blood of Christ and placeth them into the Tabernacle [Kyvot] upon the holy Altar-Table[^226] until the day of celebration of the Presanctified Liturgy.

§. 145. (b) Vespers, as a Part of the Holy Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts.

The Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts is celebrated on Wednesday and Friday of all weeks of Great Lent, on Thursday of the Great Canon (in the 5th week), on Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday of Passion Week, and if the parish feast or the day of the 40 Martyrs should fall on a weekday of the fast.[^227]

On the appointed day the priest vests in all the sacred vestments, of bright or dark color, praying only: “Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy” over each vestment, and kisseth the vestments.

Having opened the curtain (the Royal Doors, however, remaining closed), the priest maketh a bow before the holy Altar-Table, kisseth the Gospel Book and the Altar-Table, taketh the Gospel Book with both hands, and having signed the Altar-Table crosswise, exclaimeth: “Blessed is the kingdom of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages.” The choir: “Amen.”

> **Reader:** “Come, let us worship” (thrice), and the Introductory Psalm 103: “Bless the Lord, O my soul.” The priest, having gone forth through the northern door, standing before the Royal Doors readeth the prayers of the Lucernarium (beginning from the 4th, because the first three prayers are read later at the litanies of the faithful). After the psalm the priest entereth into the holy Sanctuary [Altar], and exclaimeth the Great Litany: “In peace let us pray to the Lord.” After the litany the reader readeth the 18th Kathisma: “Unto the Lord in mine affliction have I cried...” divided into three Stases [Slavas] At the first Stasis [Slava] the priest taketh the Holy Lamb from the Tabernacle [Kyvot], placeth it upon the Diskos, and censeth the holy Altar-Table round about and the Holy Lamb.

At the second Stasis [Slava] the priest, having censed the Altar-Table, taketh the Diskos with the Holy Lamb and, having placed it upon his head, transferreth it through the northern door unto the Table of Preparation [Prothesis], a candle-bearer preceding him with a candle and censer. Having arrived at the Table of Preparation [Prothesis], he setteth down the holy Diskos, poureth wine and water into the chalice, censeth the Asterisk [Star] and the veils, covereth the Diskos and the chalice each with its veil separately, and thereafter both together with the Aer, praying: “Let us pray to the Lord” over each, and censeth the oblation, saying nothing.

At the third Stasis [Slava] the priest returneth unto the holy Altar-Table, foldeth the Iliton, and censeth the Altar-Table round about.

After the Kathisma there is the small litany. After the litany: “Lord, I have cried” is sung with stichera on 10. At the last sticheron the Royal Doors are opened and the entrance is made with the censer (and if the Gospel Book is to be read, then with the holy Gospel Book). The priest kisseth the holy Altar-Table, goeth forth through the northern door before the Royal Doors, and exclaimeth: “Wisdom! Stand aright!” The choir singeth: “O Joyful Light.”

After “O Joyful Light” the priest entereth into the holy Sanctuary [Altar], and standing before the holy Altar-Table saith: “Let us attend!” “Peace be unto all.” “Wisdom! Let us attend!” The reader exclaimeth the first Prokimenon of the day. The priest: “Wisdom!” The reader: the title of the first Paremia (from the Book of Genesis). The priest: “Let us attend!” The reader readeth the Paremia. The Royal Doors are closed.

After the first Paremia the Royal Doors are opened. The priest exclaimeth: “Let us attend! Wisdom!” The reader exclaimeth the second Prokimenon of the day. After the Prokimenon the reader saith: “Command!” And the priest, having taken with both hands the candlestick with the lighted candle and the censer, standing before the holy Altar-Table and turning toward the east, signeth crosswise and exclaimeth: “Wisdom! Stand aright!” After this, turning toward the west unto the people, he exclaimeth: “The light of Christ enlighteneth all!” And the people prostrate themselves unto the ground. The priest, having returned unto the holy Altar-Table, setteth down the candlestick and the censer. The Royal Doors are closed. The reader readeth the second Paremia (from the Book of Proverbs). After the Paremia the priest exclaimeth: “Peace be unto thee.” The reader: “And unto thy spirit.”

And immediately the Royal Doors are opened. The priest, having taken the censer and standing before the holy Altar-Table, singeth with a contrite voice:[^228]

“Let my prayer arise like incense before Thee, the lifting up of my hands as an evening sacrifice.” The choir singeth the very same, and all the people pray kneeling. And the priest censeth the holy Altar-Table on the right side.

Then the priest singeth the 1st verse: “Lord, I have cried unto Thee, hear me; attend to the voice of my supplication, when I cry unto Thee.” The choir: “Let my prayer arise...” And the priest censeth the High Place.

Then the priest singeth the 2nd verse: “Set a guard, O Lord, upon my mouth, and a door of enclosure about my lips.” The choir: “Let my prayer arise...” The priest censeth the holy Altar-Table on the left side.

Then the priest singeth the 3rd verse: “Incline not my heart unto words of malice, to devise excuses for sins.” The choir: “Let my prayer arise...” The priest censeth the holy Altar-Table on the front side.

Then the priest singeth the 4th verse: “With men that work iniquity, and I will not join with their chosen ones.” The choir: “Let my prayer arise...” The priest departeth unto the Table of Preparation [Prothesis] and censeth the offered Holy Gifts.

Then the priest singeth: “The righteous man shall correct me in mercy and reprove me, but let not the oil of the sinner anoint my head.” The choir: “Let my prayer arise...”

Then the priest, having returned before the holy Altar-Table, singeth the first half of the verse: “Let my prayer arise like incense before Thee.” And having handed over the censer, he prostrateth himself unto the ground. And the choir finisheth singing: “The lifting up of my hands as an evening sacrifice.” The priest maketh 3 prostrations with the prayer of St. Ephrem: “O Lord and Master of my life...”[^229] The Royal Doors are closed.

(If it is a feast day, then after “Let my prayer arise” the Epistle and Gospel of the feast are read.)

And hereby Vespers as a part of the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts is concluded.

§. 146. (c) The Liturgy of the Catechumens.

Thereafter the Royal Doors are opened, and the priest readeth the Litany of Fervent Supplication: “Let us all say with our whole soul and with our whole mind, let us say...” The choir singeth: “Lord, have mercy” (thrice each time). After the litany the priest exclaimeth: “For a merciful and man-befriending God art Thou, and unto Thee we ascribe glory: to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages.” The choir: “Amen.”

After this the deacon or priest exclaimeth: “Pray unto the Lord, ye catechumens.” The choir: “Lord, have mercy.” “Ye faithful, let us pray for the catechumens...” and the priest prayeth quietly the prayer for the catechumens. After the litany the exclamation: “That they also with us may glorify Thine all-honorable and majestic name...”

From Wednesday of the 4th week of Great Lent (Mid-Pentecost of the fast), after the Litany of Fervent Supplication there is added also the Litany for Those Preparing for Illumination (for those catechumens who prepare for Holy Baptism on Great Saturday, on which account they were called the illuminated—*illuminandi*): “Pray, ye that are preparing for illumination, unto the Lord...” And the priest quietly prayeth the prayer for those preparing for illumination: “Manifest, O Lord, Thy countenance...” After the litany the exclamation: “For Thou art our illumination, and unto Thee we ascribe glory: to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages.”

After this he exclaimeth: “All ye that are preparing for illumination, depart... All ye who are catechumens, depart; let none of the catechumens remain; as many as are of the faithful, again and again in peace let us pray to the Lord.”

And hereby the Liturgy of the Catechumens is concluded.

§. 147. (d) The Liturgy of the Faithful.

The Liturgy of the Faithful beginneth with two small litanies: 1. “Again and again in peace let us pray to the Lord”; the priest readeth the 1st Prayer of the Faithful: “O God, great and praiseworthy...” and exclaimeth: “For unto Thee is due all glory, honor, and worship...” The choir: “Amen.” 2. “Again and again...”; the priest readeth the 2nd Prayer of the Faithful: “O holy Master, exceedingly good...” and the exclamation: “According to the gift of Thy Christ...” The choir: “Amen.”

After these litanies the Royal Doors are opened, and the priest censeth the holy Altar-Table round about, reciting Psalm 50. The choir then beginneth to sing the magnificent hymn:

“Now the heavenly hosts minister invisibly with us: for behold, the King of Glory entereth; behold, the mystical sacrifice, fully accomplished, is ushered in with spears. In faith and love let us draw near, that we may become partakers of life everlasting. Alleluia, alleluia, alleluia.”

During this hymn the priest prayeth before the holy Altar-Table, saying thrice with bows: “Now the heavenly hosts...” After this, having kissed the Altar-Table, he departeth unto the Table of Preparation [Prothesis], a candle-bearer preceding him with a candle and censer. Having censed the gifts, the priest taketh the Aer and placeth it upon his left shoulder, taketh the Diskos into his right hand and the chalice into his left hand before his breast, and saying nothing goeth forth through the northern door amidst profound silence. The people and the singers fall prostrate upon the earth with fear and reverence, because the Holy Gifts are being transferred, already consecrated and transubstantiated into the true Body and true Blood of Christ the Savior.

The priest, having passed through the church, entereth through the Royal Doors into the holy Sanctuary [Altar], setteth the holy Diskos and the holy chalice upon the antimension (iliton), removeth the veils, and covereth them with the Aer, and censeth them thrice, saying nothing.

The choir then singeth the second half of the hymn: “In faith and love let us draw near...” Upon the conclusion of this singing, all arise from the earth; the priest maketh 3 prostrations with the prayer of St. Ephrem: “O Lord and Master of my life...” The Royal Doors are closed, and the curtain is drawn halfway.

After this the priest exclaimeth the Litany of Supplication: “Let us complete our evening prayer unto the Lord.” The choir: “Lord, have mercy.” “For the precious Gifts set forth and presanctified...” And the priest prayeth quietly: “O God of ineffable and invisible mysteries...” After the litany the exclamation: “And account us worthy, O Master, with boldness...” The choir singeth: “Our Father.” The priest: “For Thine is the kingdom...” The choir: “Amen.” The priest: “Peace be unto all.” The choir: “And unto thy spirit.” The priest: “Bow your heads unto the Lord.” The choir: “To Thee, O Lord.” The priest quietly saith the prayer of the bowing of heads: “O God, Who alone art good and compassionate...” and the exclamation: “Through the grace and compassions...” The choir: “Amen.”

The priest prayeth quietly: “Attend, O Lord Jesus Christ our God...” After the prayer he boweth thrice, saying: “O God, be merciful unto me a sinner!” and having inserted his hand beneath the Aer, toucheth the Holy Bread with reverence. And immediately the curtain is drawn completely, and the priest elevateth the Holy Lamb, saying: “The Presanctified holy things for the holy!” The choir singeth: “One is holy, one is Lord, Jesus Christ, to the glory of God the Father. Amen.”

And it singeth the Communion Hymn of the day: “O taste and see that the Lord is good. Alleluia” (thrice).

The priest breaketh the Holy Lamb into 4 parts in the customary manner, and placeth the particle “IC” into the holy chalice, saying nothing. The server poureth in the zeon [warm water]. The priest receiveth communion of the particles of the Body and Blood of Christ, just as at the full Liturgy, but from the chalice he drinketh saying nothing.[^230]

After communion the curtain and the Royal Doors are opened, and the priest showeth the holy chalice unto the people, exclaiming: “With the fear of God and with faith draw near!” The choir: “I will bless the Lord at all times; His praise shall continually be in my mouth.” The priest blesseth the people, saying: “O God, save Thy people, and bless Thine inheritance.” The choir: “Taste of the heavenly bread and the cup of life, and see that the Lord is good. Alleluia” (thrice).

The priest carrieth the Holy Gifts unto the Table of Preparation [Prothesis]. Having returned unto the Altar-Table, he exclaimeth: “Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages.” The choir: “May our mouths be filled with Thy praise...” After this the thanksgiving litany: “Stand aright! Having partaken...” The priest foldeth the antimension, taketh the holy Gospel Book, and maketh therewith a cross over the antimension, exclaiming: “For Thou art our sanctification...” The choir: “Amen.” The priest: “Let us go forth in peace.” The choir: “In the name of the Lord.” The deacon: “Let us pray to the Lord.” The choir: “Lord, have mercy.”

The priest goeth forth from the Sanctuary [Altar], and standing before the ambo readeth the Prayer Behind the Ambo of the Presanctified Liturgy: “O Master Almighty, Who hast fashioned all creation in wisdom...” After the prayer the choir singeth: “Amen,” and “Blessed be the name of the Lord from this time forth, and unto the ages” (thrice); and the priest, having returned into the holy Sanctuary [Altar], readeth the prayer at the consumption of the Holy Gifts: “O Lord our God, Who hast led us into these all-honorable days...” He consumeth the Holy Gifts, washeth his hands, divesteth himself of the vestments, readeth the prayers of thanksgiving, and maketh the dismissal.

[...Blessed be the] name [of the Lord,”] the choir readeth Psalm 33 (“I will bless the Lord”), and upon the conclusion of this reading exclaimeth: “It is truly right... of our [God],” unto which the priest exclaimeth: “Wisdom!” and the choir continueth: “More honorable than the Cherubim,” etc., and there is the dismissal and blessing. After the dismissal the priest saith: “Now lettest Thou Thy servant depart,” etc., and the prayers found in the Sluzhebnik. Finally, having kissed the holy Altar-Table and the Cross, he departeth from the holy Altar-Table and, having divested himself of the priestly vestments, washeth his hands, readeth the prayers after Holy Communion, and having given thanks unto God, departeth.

### CHAPTER II.

§. 148.

Concerning Liturgies on Various Occasions.

It was already stated that the Greek Church in a single Horologion combineth the Oktoechos with the Menaion, that is, the celebration of an immovable feast with the celebration of a movable feast, because she celebrateth every day a twofold feast—and from this combination there have arisen various occurrences. So it is likewise concerning the holy Liturgy. But here the variety of these occurrences is further increased also by the fact that here with the daily Liturgy there can be combined also a Liturgy of various intentions, as for example: for every petition, or for the remission of sins, or for the sick, etc. But besides this there are also certain extraordinary occurrences in holy Liturgies. And in accordance herewith we shall speak here: (α) concerning regular occurrences, and (β) concerning the most notable extraordinary occurrences.

#### ARTICLE I.

Concerning Regular Occurrences.

§. 149.

I. How the Liturgy is Celebrated if it is Sunday and a Minor Saint?

At the Liturgy the typical antiphons are sung, that is, “Bless the Lord, O my soul” (Psalm 102) and “Praise the Lord, O my soul” (Psalm 145). The first is concluded with “Glory... now and forever,” and the second after “Glory and now and forever” with the hymn “Only-begotten Son,” etc. At the Beatitudes, the Sunday stichera of the Oktoechos on 8. After the entrance, the Sunday troparion; “Glory”: the Sunday kontakion; “Now and forever”: the kontakion according to the tone from the feasts of the Mother of God,[^231] or of the temple, if the temple is of Christ or of the Mother of God,[^232] or “O Protection of Christians,” if the temple is of a Saint.[^233] The Prokimenon of the tone. The Epistle and Gospel of the Sunday. The Communion Hymn likewise of the Sunday: “Praise the Lord.” From this it is evident that a Minor Saint hath no proper celebration at the Liturgy.

§. 150.

II. How the Liturgy is Celebrated if it is Sunday and a Saint on 6.

Among these Saints there are such as have a proper celebration, and then the Liturgy is celebrated thus: The typical antiphons; stichera at the Beatitudes of the tone of the Oktoechos on 6, and of the Saint Ode 3 on 4. After the entrance, the troparia are taken in such wise that first the Sunday troparion is sung, thereafter the troparion of the Saint, the Sunday kontakion; “Glory”: the kontakion of the Saint; “Now and forever”: the Theotokion as stated above. The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn of the Sunday in course, and—if the Saint likewise hath them—then they are taken also for him.

§. 151.

III. How the Liturgy is Celebrated if it is Sunday and a Polyeleos Saint.

“Bless the Lord, O my soul,” etc. The Beatitudes of the tone in course on 6, and unto the Saint Ode 3 on 4. After the entrance, the Sunday troparion and the troparion of the Saint; the Sunday kontakion; “Glory”: the kontakion of the Saint; “Now and forever”: the kontakion according to the tone from the feasts of the Mother of God, or “O Protection of Christians.” The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn of the Sunday and of the Saint.

§. 152.

IV. How the Liturgy is Celebrated if it is Sunday and a Forefeast of a Feast of the Lord or of the Mother of God?

The typical antiphons are taken; the Beatitudes of the Oktoechos on 6, and of the Forefeast Ode 3 on 4. After the entrance, the Sunday Troparion and the Troparion of the Forefeast; “Glory”: the Sunday Kontakion; “Now and forever”: the Kontakion of the Forefeast. The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn are taken of the Sunday alone.

§. 153.

V. How the Liturgy is Celebrated if it is Sunday and a Feast of the Mother of God?

“Bless the Lord, O my soul,” and the Sunday Beatitudes on 6, and of the feast Ode 3 on 4. After the entrance, the Sunday troparion and the troparion of the feast; “Glory”: the Sunday kontakion; “Now and forever”: the kontakion of the feast. The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn of the Sunday and of the Feast. At the Megalynarion [“Especially”], instead of “It is truly right,” the refrain and Heirmos of the 9th Ode of the Feast.

§. 154.

VI. How the Liturgy is Celebrated if it is Sunday and a Feast of the Lord?

Festal antiphons are taken.[^234] After each verse of the 1st festal Antiphon there is appended the refrain: “Through the prayers of the Mother of God, O Savior, save us”; after each verse of the 2nd Antiphon there is appended the refrain: “Save us, O Son of God, Who art risen from the dead” (or “Who wast born of the Virgin,” or “Who wast transfigured on the mount,” corresponding to the feast), “who chant unto Thee: Alleluia.” After the 2nd Antiphon: “Glory... now and forever,” “Only-begotten Son.” After each verse of the 3rd Antiphon the troparion of the feast is sung. Likewise instead of “Come, let us worship,” the great feasts have an Introit [Entrance Hymn], that is, entrance verses of the feast taken from the prophetic Psalms pertaining unto the feast. After the entrance, the troparion of the Feast; “Glory... now and forever”: the kontakion of the Feast. At the Megalynarion [“Especially”], instead of “It is truly right,” the refrain of the feast is sung with the Heirmos of the 9th Ode. The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn of the Feast; of the Sunday nothing is taken.[^235]

Notes: 1) If it is Sunday, a Minor Saint, and a service of intention:
After the entrance: the troparion of the Sunday and the troparion of intention, the kontakion of intention; “Glory”: the Sunday kontakion; “Now and forever”: as above (that is, if the temple is of Christ or of the Mother of God, the kontakion of the temple; if of a Saint: “O Protection of Christians” or from the Theotokion kontakia). The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn first of the Sunday, and of intention. (Unto the Minor Saint nothing is taken.)
2) If it is Sunday, a Great Saint, and a service of intention:
After the entrance: the Sunday troparion, the troparion of the Saint, and the troparion of intention; the kontakion of intention; “Glory”: the kontakion unto the Saint; “Now and forever”: as above.
3) If it is Sunday, a Feast of the Lord, and a service of intention:
After the entrance: the troparion of the feast and of intention; “Glory”: the kontakion of intention; “Now and forever”: the kontakion of the feast. (Of the Sunday nothing is taken.) The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn of the feast and of intention.

§. 155.

VII. How the Liturgy is Celebrated if it is Sunday and within a Feast there is a Minor Saint?

Unto the Minor Saint nothing is taken, but only unto the Sunday and the Afterfeast. There are sung: the typical antiphons; stichera at the Beatitudes of the tone on 6, and of the feast the ode in course from the canon on 4. After the entrance, if it is an afterfeast of a Feast of the Lord, after “Wisdom! Stand aright!”: “Come, let us worship” and “Save us, O Son of God, Who wast born of the Virgin,” or “Who wast transfigured on the mount,” etc. After the entrance, the Sunday troparion, the troparion of the feast; “Glory”: the Sunday kontakion; “Now and forever”: the kontakion of the feast. The Prokimenon of the tone and of the feast. The Epistle and Gospel of the Sunday only. Instead of “It is truly right,” the refrain of the feast with the Heirmos of the 9th Ode. The Communion Hymn of the Sunday and of the Feast.

§. 156.

VIII. How the Liturgy is Celebrated if it is Sunday and within a Feast there is a Middle Saint having a Celebration?

The typical antiphons. The Beatitudes of the tone on 4, of the feast the ode in course from the canon on 4, and of the Saint Ode 3 on 4. After the entrance, if it is an afterfeast of a Feast of the Lord, there is sung “Come, let us worship,” and the final refrain of the feast. After the entrance: the Sunday troparion, the troparion of the Saint, and the troparion of the feast; likewise the Sunday kontakion; “Glory”: the kontakion of the Saint; “Now and forever”: the kontakion of the feast. The Prokimenon of the tone and of the Saint. The Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn of the Sunday and of the Saint. Instead of “It is truly right,” the refrain of the feast and the Heirmos of the 9th Ode of the canon are sung.

§. 157.

IX. How the Liturgy is Celebrated if it is Sunday and within a Feast there is a Great Saint?

The typical antiphons; stichera at the Beatitudes of the Oktoechos on 4, of the feast the ode in course on 4, and of the Saint Ode 3 on 4. At the entrance, if it is an afterfeast of a Feast of the Lord, there is sung: “Come, let us worship” and the final refrain of the feast. After the entrance: the Sunday troparion, the troparion of the feast, and the troparion of the Saint; “Glory”: the kontakion of the Saint; “Now and forever”: the kontakion of the feast. The Prokimenon of the tone and of the Saint. The Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn of the Sunday and of the Saint.[^236] Instead of “It is truly right,” the refrain of the feast.

§. 158.

X. How the Liturgy is Celebrated if the Apodosis of a Feast Falleth on a Sunday?

The typical antiphons. The Beatitudes of the tone on 6, and of the feast Ode 9 on 4, that is, unto 4 verses of the Beatitudes the 4 troparia of the 9th Ode of the Matins canon prescribed for the feast are appended. At the entrance, if it is the Apodosis of a Feast of the Lord, there is sung: “Come, let us worship” and the final refrain of the feast. After the entrance: the Sunday troparion and the troparion of the feast;

“Glory”: the Sunday kontakion; “Now and forever”: the kontakion of the feast. The Epistle and Gospel of the Sunday in course only. Instead of “It is truly right,” the Heirmos of the 9th Ode of the feast is sung. The Prokimenon and Communion Hymn of the Sunday and of the Feast. The dismissal of the Sunday.

§. 159.

XI. How the Liturgy is Celebrated if the Patronal Feast of a Saint Falleth with a Forefeast or with the Apodosis of a Feast on a Sunday?

The typical antiphons; stichera at the Beatitudes: the Sunday ones on 4, of the feast Ode 3 on 4, of the temple Ode 6 on 4. After the entrance, the troparion of the Sunday, of the feast, and of the temple of the Saint; the Sunday kontakion; “Glory”: the kontakion of the temple; “Now and forever”: of the feast. The Prokimenon of the tone and of the temple. The Epistle in course and of the temple. The Gospel in course and of the temple. The Communion Hymn of the Sunday and of the temple.

Notes: 1) If it is Sunday, within a feast a Minor Saint, and a service of intention:
After the entrance: the Sunday troparion, the troparion of the feast, and the troparion of intention; “Glory”: the kontakion of intention; “Now and forever”: of the feast. The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn of the Sunday and of intention.
2) If it is Sunday, within a feast a Great Saint, and a service of intention:
After the entrance: the Sunday troparion, the troparion of the feast, the troparion of the Saint, and the troparion of intention; the kontakion of intention; “Glory”: the kontakion of the Saint; “Now and forever”: of the feast. The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn of the Sunday, unto the Saint, and of intention.
3) If it is Sunday, the Apodosis of a feast, and a service of intention:
After the entrance: the Sunday troparion, the troparion of the feast, and the troparion of intention; “Glory”: the kontakion of intention; “Now and forever”: the kontakion of the feast.

§. 160.

XII. How the Holy Liturgy is Celebrated if it is a Weekday and a Minor Saint?

The weekday service according to the Oktoechos is taken on Monday unto the Angels, on Tuesday unto the Forerunner, on Wednesday and Friday unto the Cross, on Thursday unto the Apostles and Saint Nicholas, and on Saturday unto All Saints. But the service unto those Saints at the Liturgy is taken only then, when upon a weekday there falleth a Minor or Middle Saint.[^237]
On such days the daily antiphons are taken (1st Antiphon: “It is good to give praise”; 2nd Antiphon: “The Lord reigneth”; 3rd Antiphon: “Come, let us rejoice in the Lord”).[^238]
After the entrance (if the service is taken for the day alone): The troparion of the day; “Glory”: the kontakion of the day; “Now and forever”: the kontakion from the feasts of the Mother of God according to the tone, or the kontakion of the temple, if the temple is of Christ or of the Mother of God; but if the temple is of a Saint, at “Now and forever”: “O Protection of Christians.”[^239] The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn of the day. (Unto the Minor Saint nothing is taken.)
Note. With a service of intention it is taken thus: The troparion of the day and the troparion of intention; the kontakion of intention; “Glory”: the kontakion of the day; “Now and forever”: “O Protection of Christians” (or the kontakion of the temple, where the temple is of Christ or of the Mother of God).[^240] The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn of the day [and] of intention.

§. 161.

XIII. How the Liturgy is Celebrated on a Weekday if there is a Saint on 6.

The daily antiphons, unless the Saint hath [stichera] at the Beatitudes, otherwise the typical antiphons. After the entrance: the troparion of the day and the troparion of the Saint (if he hath an Epistle and Gospel), the kontakion of the day; “Glory”: the kontakion of the Saint; “Now and forever”: the kontakion as above. The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn of the day and of the Saint, if he hath them.[^241]

Note. If there is a service of a particular intention, there is sung: the troparion of the day, the troparion of the Saint, and the troparion of intention; the kontakion of the day, the kontakion of the Saint; “Glory”: the kontakion of intention; “Now and forever”: the Theotokion, as above. On Wednesday or Friday it is covered with the daily kontakion of the Cross.

§. 162.

XIV. How the Liturgy is Celebrated if a Great Saint Falleth on a Weekday.

When on a weekday (other than Sunday) a Great Saint falleth, then the entire daily service—the troparion with the kontakion, the Prokimenon with the Communion Hymn—is omitted at the Liturgy. The Epistle and Gospel in course (that is, the daily series [*stolp*]) are read one day beforehand, and the entire service is taken unto the Saint.[^242] The typical antiphons. After the entrance: the troparion unto the Saint; “Glory”: the kontakion unto the Saint; “Now and forever”: “O Protection of Christians,” or the kontakion of the temple, where the temple is of Christ or of the Mother of God. The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn unto the Saint.[^243]
Note. If the priest hath a particular intention, then: the troparion of the Saint and the troparion of intention; the kontakion of intention; “Glory”: the kontakion of the Saint; “Now and forever”: as above. The Prokimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and Communion Hymn unto the Saint and of intention.

§. 163.

XV. What We Need to Note Concerning Weekday Liturgies Celebrated during the Holy Pentecostarion (That Is, from Thomas Sunday until the Sunday of All Saints).[^244]

Since those Sundays are festal, whose entire service is repeated also on weekdays, therefore the troparia, kontakia, prokimena, and communion hymns of those Sunday services are likewise repeated on weekdays at the liturgical services. The Epistles and Gospels are read daily in course, corresponding to the festal Sundays. Concerning these Liturgies we note the following:

1) At a weekday Liturgy during the Holy Pentecostarion with the service of a Minor Saint:
(α) Without an intention: The typical antiphons and the Beatitudes of the feast, the ode in course. After the entrance: the Sunday troparion (festal, of the resurrection),[^245] “Glory... now and forever”: the festal kontakion (of the Sunday).[^246] The Prokimenon and Communion Hymn of the festal Sunday: “Praise the Lord”:[^247] In place of “It is truly right”: “The Angel cried aloud,” and at Mid-Pentecost: the Heirmos of the 9th Ode until the Apodosis.
(β) With an intention: The Sunday troparion and the troparion of intention; “Glory”: the kontakion of intention; “Now and forever”: the festal kontakion. The Prokimenon, Epistle, and Gospel in course and of intention. The Communion Hymn: “Praise the Lord” and of intention. In place of “It is truly right”: “The Angel cried aloud,” and at Mid-Pentecost: the Heirmos of Mid-Pentecost.

2) At a weekday Liturgy during the Holy Pentecostarion with the service of a Polyeleos Saint:
(α) Without an intention: The troparion of the Sunday and the troparion of the Saint; “Glory”: the kontakion unto the Saint; “Now and forever”: the festal kontakion (of the Sunday). The Prokimenon, Epistle, and Gospel in course and unto the Saint. The Communion Hymn: “Praise the Lord” and unto the Saint.
