After the Little Entrance and after the singing of the troparia, when the choir singeth: “Many Years,” the one being ordained is led by the consecrators before the Royal Doors; here the Hierarch Consecrator layeth upon him the epigonation [palitza / nabedrennik], saying: “Gird Thy sword upon Thy thigh, O Mighty One... Axios”; and the choir singeth: “Axios,” three times.

After this the Bishop Consecrator, delivering unto the one being ordained (p. 250)

the pastoral staff, speaketh: “Receive this staff... Axios”; and the choir singeth: “Axios.”

After this the one being ordained falleth upon his knees before the holy Altar-Table, and layeth upon it his hands and his head. The consecrators hold the open holy Gospel-Book over his head with the writing turned downward; while the Hierarch Consecrator layeth his right hand upon his head and readeth the prayer of ordination: “Divine grace... promoteth the reverend... unto a Bishop...” The choir singeth three times: “Lord, have mercy.” — The Hierarch readeth the secret prayers, while the consecrators speak the litany.

After this followeth the investiture with all the hierarchal vestments: the sakkos, the omophorion, the panagia, the pectoral cross, and the mitre, with the singing of: “Axios.”

After the investiture the Hierarch Consecrator and all the consecrators give unto the newly-ordained Bishop the fraternal kiss (the kiss of peace).

After this the newly-ordained Bishop is led up unto the high place, where he sitteth as a Bishop during the reading of the Epistle.

After the Ambo Prayer the newly-ordained Bishop maketh the circuit of the church, blessing the people on both sides, while the choir singeth: “Many Years.”

(p. 251)

After this the newly-ordained Bishop goeth into the sacristy, where the liturgical vestments are removed from him, and he is clothed in the hierarchal ryasa and mantle.

§. 182.

The Order of the Holy Mystery of Matrimony.[^289]

I. The Order of the Blessing of the Matrimony of the First-Married.

Before the priest proceedeth unto the blessing of the marriage, he must assure himself concerning the free status of the bridegroom and the bride, concerning their mutual consent, concerning the completion of the lawful banns, and whether there be any canonical impediment (*impedimentum*) unto the marriage. Likewise the bride and bridegroom must approach unto the Holy Mystery of Repentance and Holy Communion.

On the appointed day and hour the bridegroom and bride, accompanied by witnesses (groomsmen and bridesmaids) and parents, come into the church, and take their stand before the tetrapod: the bridegroom on the right hand, and the bride on the left hand. Upon the tetrapod are laid the holy cross, the holy Gospel-Book, two crowns, and two candles.

The priest, vested in epitrachelion and phelonion, cometh out before the Royal Doors or unto the tetrapod, and questioneth the bridegroom: “Hast thou a good and unconstrained desire, and a firm purpose, to take unto thyself as wife this woman (*Name*)?” The bridegroom answereth: “I have, reverend Father.” Further he questioneth: “Hast thou not promised thyself unto another bride?” He answereth: “I have not promised myself, reverend Father.” In like manner he questioneth the bride also.

(p. 252)

After this the priest giveth into their hands lighted candles, and beginneth the betrothal, saying: “Blessed is our God...” Cantor: “Amen.” There followeth the Great Litany with special petitions for those being betrothed: “That this betrothal may be blessed...” After the litany the priest readeth two prayers, blesseth the rings, and layeth them upon the Altar-Table or upon the tetrapod. After this he taketh the ring of the bridegroom, maketh with it a cross over the head of the bridegroom, saying three times: “The servant of God (*Name*) is betrothed unto the handmaid of God (*Name*), in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit. Amen.” And he placeth the ring upon a finger of his right hand. In like manner he maketh also for the bride. After this the groomsmen exchange the rings three times. The priest readeth the prayer: “O Lord our God, Who didst accompany the servant of the Patriarch Abraham...” And there followeth the Augmented Litany and the dismissal of the betrothal.

After this followeth the crowning itself.

The priest goeth before them with the censer unto the analoj [tetrapod], while the cantors sing Psalm 127 LXX [Psalm 128 MT]: “Blessed are all they that fear the Lord...” with the refrain: “Glory to Thee, our God, glory to Thee.”

Having arrived at the tetrapod, the bridegroom and bride lay their right hands upon the holy Gospel-Book, while the priest bindeth their hands with the epitrachelion, blesseth them with the cross, and they pronounce the marital oath of mutual fidelity, love, and the indissolubility of the union even unto death.

After this the priest beginneth: “Blessed is the Kingdom...” The Great Litany. Three long prayers. After the prayers the priest taketh the crown of the bridegroom, signeth the bridegroom cruciformly with it, and crowneth him, saying three times: “The servant of God (*Name*) is crowned unto the handmaid of God (*Name*), in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit. Amen.” In like manner he crowneth the bride also. After this he blesseth both three times, saying: “O Lord our God, crown them with glory and honor!”

There followeth the Prokimenon, the Epistle (Eph. § 230), the Gospel (John § 6), the Augmented Litany and the Litany of Supplication. “Our Father.”

(p. 253)

After “Our Father” there is offered the common cup with blessed wine, from which the bridegroom and bride drink three times. After this the priest bindeth their hands with the epitrachelion, and leadeth them three times around the tetrapod, while the cantors sing the troparia: “Rejoice, O Isaiah...”, “O Holy Martyrs...”, “Glory to Thee, O Christ God...”

After this the priest removeth the crowns from the bridegroom and bride, saying unto each the corresponding greeting: “Be thou magnified, O bridegroom, as Abraham...” and: “And thou, O bride, be thou magnified as Sarah...” He readeth two prayers for the removal of the crowns. After this there followeth the Augmented Litany and the full festal dismissal: “He Who in Cana of Galilee...” The newlyweds kiss the cross and receive congratulations from those present.

II. The Order of the Blessing of the Matrimony of the Second-Married.

If both (the bridegroom and bride) are second-married (widowed), it is celebrated according to a special order having a penitential character. The betrothal is not celebrated separately, but together with the crowning.

The priest vesteth only in epitrachelion and phelonion. Beginning: “Blessed is our God...” Trisagion. “O Most Holy Trinity...” “Our Father...” Troparion of the day. There followeth the litany: “In peace let us pray unto the Lord,” with the addition of petitions for the remission of sins and cleansing of the iniquities of the bridegroom and bride.

After the litany the priest readeth two prayers with penitential meaning: “O Eternal God, Who hast gathered together the dispersed into union...” and the second prayer: “O Lord our God, Who desirest that all men be saved...”

After this he placeth the rings without special prayers, but directly with the words: “The servant of God is betrothed...”

(p. 254)

After this he layeth on the crowns with the words: “The servant of God is crowned...” and blesseth: “O Lord our God, crown them with glory and honor!” He readeth two prayers: “O Master, Lord our God, Who hast mercy upon all men...” and: “O Lord Jesus Christ, Word of God...”

After this the common cup is offered, the procession around the tetrapod with the singing of: “Rejoice, O Isaiah...”, the removal of crowns with the corresponding prayers.

The litany: “Have mercy on us, O God...” and the dismissal.[^290]

Note. If, however, only one of those being married is second-married (a widower or a widow), and the other first-married (a bachelor or a maiden), then the order of the first-married is celebrated, with all solemnities.

§. 183.

The Order of the Holy Mystery of Holy Anointing [Eleosvyashcheniye].

This holy Mystery is celebrated over the severely ill by a synod of seven priests (whence also the name “Soborovanie”), but in case of need it can be celebrated also by a smaller number of priests, even by a single priest.

In the house of the sick person there is prepared a table covered with a white cloth. Upon the table is set a vessel with wheat (or rye), and upon the wheat is placed a lamp (or cup) with oil mixed with wine. Around the vessel are set 7 small wands wrapped with cotton [struptsy] for the anointing, and 7 lighted candles.

The priests vest in epitrachelia and phelonia (if, however, there be one priest, then in epitrachelion and phelonion).

(p. 255)

The usual beginning: “Blessed is our God...” Trisagion. “Our Father.” Psalm 142 LXX [Psalm 143 MT]: “O Lord, hear my prayer...” The Little Litany. Penitential troparia: “Have mercy on us, O Lord, have mercy on us...” Psalm 50 LXX [Psalm 51 MT]: “Have mercy upon me, O God...” The Canon of Holy Anointing, the composition of Patriarch Arsenius, in Tone 4: “O bountiful and merciful Lord...” After the 9th Ode, the stichera and the troparion: “O Christ, Who alone art swift to help...”

There followeth the Great Litany of Holy Anointing: “In peace let us pray unto the Lord... That this oil may be blessed...” After the litany the presiding priest readeth the prayer over the oil: “O Lord, Who by Thy mercy and bounties healest the contrite of our souls and bodies, do Thou Thyself bless this oil also...”

After this followeth the 7-fold reading of the Epistle, the Gospel, the prayer, and the 7-fold anointing of the sick person.[^291]

The 1st Epistle from James (§ 57: “Is any sick among you...”). The Gospel from Luke (§ 51: concerning the Good Samaritan). The 1st Prayer: “O Father without beginning, eternal, Holy of Holies...” After the prayer the priest taketh a wand, dippeth it into the holy oil, and anointeth the sick person cruciformly upon the forehead, the nostrils, the cheeks, the lips, the breast, and upon both hands,[^292] saying the form of the mystery: “O Holy Father, Physician of souls and bodies... [Forma Sacramenti] heal Thy servant (*Name*) from the bodily and spiritual infirmity which holdeth him...”[^293]

In like manner are celebrated also the second, third, fourth, fifth, sixth, and seventh anointings with the corresponding Epistles, Gospels, and prayers.

(p. 256)

After the seventh anointing the sick person (if he can) himself kneeleth or sitteth, while the Priests open the holy Gospel-Book with the writing turned downward, and hold it over the head of the sick person; and the presiding Priest readeth the prayer of absolution: “O Holy King, compassionate and plenteous in mercy...” The sick person kisseth the holy Gospel-Book.

There followeth the Augmented Litany: “Have mercy on us, O God...” with the petition for the healing of the sick person, and the dismissal. The sick person asketh forgiveness from the Priests and from all his kinsfolk and relatives, saying three times: “Bless me, holy fathers, and forgive me, a sinner.” And the Priests answer: “May God forgive and heal thee.”

CHAPTER II.

Concerning the Rites of Diverse Ecclesiastical Consecrations and Blessings.[^294]

§. 184.

The Order of the Blessing of Children.

On the appointed day (usually on the Feast of the Nativity of Christ or on Theophany, or on the New Year) parents bring their children into the church.

The priest, vested in epitrachelion and phelonion, cometh out before the Royal Doors or unto the tetrapod, and blesseth: “Blessed is our God...” He readeth the usual beginning, while the cantors sing the troparion of the feast.

After the exclamation he readeth also the two prayers which be- (p. 257)

-found in the Trebnik. After the prayers he blesseth them and sprinkleth them with holy water, saying: “May the blessing of Almighty God, the Father, † and the Son, † and the Holy Spirit, † descend upon you, preserve and direct you, and abide always with you.” Amen.

An admirable and praiseworthy rite, imitating the Most Holy Virgin!

§. 185.

The Order of the Churching of a Bride after Marriage.

When the newly-married bride cometh unto the church, she standeth outside the church doors; the Priest, however, vested in the epitrachelion, cometh unto her and speaketh: “Enter, child, into the courts of the Lord,” etc.

And immediately the Priest entereth into the church with the bride and with those present. And having entered, the Priest, standing before the Icon of the Mother of God — the bride standing near him — beginneth usually: “Blessed is our God...” etc., the Trisagion, “Our Father,” “Lord, have mercy” (12 times), “Come, let us worship” (3 times), etc., and Psalm 66: “May God be merciful unto us...” etc.

After this the Priest, before the bride who hath bowed her head, proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and speaketh the prayer: “O God, our God,” and blesseth her head with his right hand, saying: “May the Lord cover thee under the shelter of His wings,” etc. And immediately he sprinkleth her with holy water, saying: “By the sprinkling of this sanctified water, may blessing descend and abide upon thee,” etc.

And after this he maketh the Dismissal; and he concludeth the rite with this blessing: “Go in peace, and diligently observe all the commandments of the Lord,” etc.

§. 186.

The Order of the Churching of a Woman after Childbirth.

When the woman cometh with the child into the church, the Priest, having taken the epitrachelion and having come unto the church doors, where the woman, having fallen upon her knees, awaiteth him, beginneth thus: “In the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, Amen.” And having laid his right hand upon the head of the woman, he speaketh first the prayer: “O Lord God Almighty...”[^295] etc., and secondly the second prayer: “O Lord our God...” etc.

(p. 258)

After these prayers he leadeth the woman into the church, saying unto her: “Enter into the house of God”; concerning the child, however, he speaketh the prayer: “The servant of God [or: the handmaid of God] is churched...” etc., which he speaketh 3 times, namely: at the doors of the church, in the midst of the church, and at the Royal Doors.

When the Priest cometh unto the Royal Doors, he sprinkleth the woman with holy water, saying: “May the peace and blessing of Almighty God, the Father, † and the Son, † and the Holy Spirit, † descend upon thee and abide always”; and the child he taketh into his arms, saying: “Now lettest Thou Thy servant depart...” etc. And thus he maketh the Dismissal.[^296]

§. 187.

The Order of the Burial of Infants.

Infants in the Church are called those children who know not yet how to speak; when, however, they begin to speak, even unto the seventh year, they are called children [*otroky*]. — The burial of infants as well as of children is celebrated in bright, that is, white vestments.

When the Priest cometh into the house where the deceased infant lieth, being vested in epitrachelion and phelonion, he putteth incense into the censer, and goeth into the room in which the deceased lieth; during this the choir singeth: “When Thou didst descend...”

After this, in the room, the Panikhida beginneth, namely: “Blessed is our God...” etc., the Trisagion, “Our Father,” “With the spirits of the righteous...”[^297] etc., and the litany: “Have mercy on us, O God,” with the exclamation. After the exclamation there is a special dismissal ending with: “In blessed repose grant eternal rest, O Lord...” etc., unto which the choir singeth: “Memory eternal.” — And the Priest, having sprinkled the body of the deceased with holy water, goeth out with the cantor from the room into the courtyard, where the burial itself beginneth.

First the Priest speaketh: “Blessed is our God...” etc.; the choir: “Amen”; and among us[^298] there follow: Psalms 112, 102, and 23 (“Praise the Lord, O ye servants...” etc., “O Lord, my heart is not exalted...” etc., “The earth is the Lord’s...” etc.).

After the Psalms the Priest proclaimeth: “For holy art Thou, O God...” etc., and the Trisagion is sung. — After this follow: the Prokeimenon, the Epis- (p. 259)

-tle, and Gospel; and there taketh place the last kiss, during which the choir singeth the stichera: “O who would not weep...” etc. The litany: “Have mercy on us, O God,” with the exclamation, and after which the Priest speaketh: “In blessed repose...” etc.; the choir: “Memory eternal,” etc.

And immediately the body of the deceased is carried unto the cemetery. On the way the “Trisagion” is sung. At the grave the Priest proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and readeth the prayer: “O Lord, Who preservest infants,” after which the body of the deceased is lowered into the grave, while the choir singeth: “Open wide, O earth, receive the body formed from thee by the hand of God...” etc. (in Tone 8). During this singing the Priest, having sealed the grave with the words: “This grave is sealed until the second coming of Christ...” etc., maketh the Dismissal.

§. 188.

The Order of the Burial of Lay Persons.

The Priest, having come into the house where the remains of the deceased lie, putteth upon himself the epitrachelion and phelonion, and having put incense into the censer, goeth into the room, while the cantor singeth: “When Thou didst descend”; in the room he beginneth the Panikhida: “Blessed is our God...” etc. After this the Trisagion, and the rest as usual. After the Dismissal the Priest speaketh: “In blessed repose...” etc., the choir singeth: “Memory eternal,” during which singing the Priest sprinkleth the body of the deceased with holy water, and with the cantor goeth out of the room.

When the body is laid upon the prepared place in the courtyard, the Priest beginneth: “Blessed is our God...” etc., and immediately among us[^299] the choir singeth “Alleluia” 3 times, and the troparia: “With depth of wisdom,” Glory, and Now: “We have thee as a wall and a haven...” etc.

After the troparia there are sung first the verses from the first stasis of Kathisma 17: “Blessed are the undefiled,” with the verse: “Blessed art Thou, O Lord, teach us Thy statutes,” etc.[^300] After this are sung verses from the second stasis of Kathisma 17: “I am Thine...” etc., with the verse: “O Savior, save the soul of Thy servant,” concluding with the sticheron: “My soul shall live...” etc. Finally the stichera “The choir of the Saints,” with the verse: “Blessed art Thou, O Lord,” and we conclude with “Alleluia” (3 times).

After this the Little Litany, and the Priest censeth the body of the deceased from the four sides.

(p. 260)

And immediately are sung the idiomela stichera of St. John of Damascus in the 8 Tones: “What earthly pleasure...” etc.

After these, among us,[^301] there are at once taken: the Prokeimenon, the Epistle, and the Gospel, after which taketh place the last kiss, when the Priest giveth the holy Cross to kiss, while the choir singeth: “Come, let us give the last kiss...” etc. The litany: “Have mercy on us, O God,” and the Priest proclaimeth: “In blessed repose...” etc. And the body is carried unto the cemetery. On the way Psalm 50 is sung. (At solemn funerals the Gospels: from John, sections [pericopes] 15, 16, 22, and 23.) At the cemetery, at the grave, the Priest proclaimeth: “Bowing our knees, let us pray to the Lord,” whereupon those present fall upon their knees, and the Priest speaketh the prayer of absolution, namely: if the deceased made confession: “O compassionate and plenteous in mercy...” etc., but if he made no confession: “O Lord Jesus Christ our God...” etc.

After the prayer of absolution they lower the body of the deceased into the grave, while the choir singeth: “Open wide, O earth,” Glory, and Now: “O Mother of the Savior of them that hope in thee.” And having lowered the deceased into the grave, the Priest sprinkleth the grave with holy water, and after this sealeth it with a shovel in such manner that, taking earth with the shovel, he casteth it cruciformly upon the remains, saying: “This grave is sealed until the second coming of Christ, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, Amen,” and thus he maketh the Dismissal. At the conclusion he speaketh: “In blessed repose, eternal rest...” etc. Memory eternal.

§. 189.

The Order of the Burial of Secular Priests.

If a certain secular Priest departeth unto the Lord, 3 Priests come and with a sponge dipped in pure oil wipe his face, and clothe him in all the priestly vestments. And having covered his face with the aer, they place the Gospel-Book at his head.

After this the Priests, having clothed themselves in epitrachelion and phelonion, come and stand round about the remains; the presiding Priest censeth the deceased and beginneth: “Blessed is our God...” etc., the Trisagion, etc., and the Panikhida concludeth with the litany: “Have mercy on us, O God,” and with the Dismissal.

When the presiding Priest proclaimeth: “In blessed repose grant eternal rest, O Lord, unto Thy departed servant (p. 261)

his...” etc., he then sprinkleth his body with holy water. — Unto which the choir: “Memory eternal.” After this, singing: “Holy God...” etc., the body is carried into the church.

In the church the body of the deceased is placed with the head toward the Sanctuary, and the face toward the people. The presiding Priest beginneth: “Blessed is our God...” etc. The verses from Kathisma 17 (“Blessed are the undefiled,” with the verse: “Blessed art Thou, O Lord, teach us Thy statutes.”) “I am Thine...” etc., with the verse: “O Savior, save the soul of Thy servant.” At the conclusion, three times: “My soul shall live...” etc. After this “The choir of the Saints,” etc., with “Alleluia,” and thus the Little Litany. Sessional hymn: “Give rest, O Savior.”

After this the Prokeimenon, the Epistle, the Gospel. Litany: “Have mercy on us, O God,” with the exclamation. Sticheron: “Today he is separated.”

And again: Prokeimenon, Epistle, and Gospel; litany: “Have mercy on us, O God...” etc.; sticheron: “The departing soul.”

In all there are 7 Epistles and Gospels with the appropriate litanies, prayers, and stichera.

After the 7th litany there is read: “In Thy Kingdom,” and thus followeth the canon: “Unto Him Who of old drowned the pursuing tyrant in the waves of the sea,” consisting of 9 odes. After the 9th ode the Exapostilarion and the stichera “O deification in the departure...” etc., which are taken unto the verses “Praise the Lord from the heavens.”

The Doxology is read, and “Vouchsafe, O Lord.” And there follow the stichera of St. John of Damascus: “What earthly pleasure...” of the 8 Tones. After this “It is good to give praise,” “Our Father,” and the Troparia: “Give rest, O Savior,” Glory: “In Thy resting place, O Lord,” Now: “Holy Mother.” And thus the litany: “Have mercy on us,” with the exclamation.

After this the last kiss with the hymn: “Come, let us give the last kiss...” etc.; at this, bowing the knees, there taketh place the prayer of absolution: “O compassionate and plenteous in mercy.” After this the Priest, standing before the remains of the deceased priest and holding the oil, saying: “May the Lord and our God Jesus Christ, Who strengthened thee in the faith...” etc. When, however, he poureth the oil upon the deceased, he speaketh: “Alleluia, alleluia, alleluia,” etc. And thus the Dismissal. Memory eternal.

Among us, besides this priestly burial, there is usually celebrated also another simple burial with the Liturgy, as was stated above.

§. 190.

The Order of Burial celebrated in Bright Week.

First of all it is proper to know that — if anyone departeth in Bright Week — the order of singing at the burial on account of (p. 262)

the majesty of the Feast of the Resurrection is in a certain manner different.

The Priest, having come into the house in which the body of the deceased lieth, and having put upon himself a bright (and not a black) phelonion and epitrachelion, goeth into the house, singing: “Christ is risen...” etc. And having censed the remains in the house, he beginneth: “Blessed is our God...” etc. The choir: “Amen,” and “Christ is risen” is sung 3 times (the Priest once, and the cantor 2 times). After this is sung unto the usual verses: “Let God arise...” etc., “Christ is risen,” “Glory, and Now,” “Christ is risen.” Litany: “Have mercy on us,” with the exclamation and the Dismissal. After this the Priest sprinkleth the body with water, while the cantor singeth: “Christ is risen.” And the remains are carried into the courtyard.[^302]

Here in the courtyard the Priest beginneth anew: “Blessed is our God...” etc., and having sung “Christ is risen,” the Paschal Canon beginneth: “The Day of Resurrection.” Between the odes are litanies. After the 6th ode the Kontakion: “With the saints give rest,” and the Ikos: “Thou alone,” and thus: “As many of you as have been baptized into Christ...” etc. Prokeimenon, Epistle, Gospel, and “Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ,” with the sticheron: “Jesus having risen from the grave.” — After this are sung the 7th, 8th, and 9th odes of the canon with the Exapostilarion: “Having fallen asleep in the flesh.” And the Sunday Troparia: “The assembly of the Angels,” with the refrain: “Blessed art Thou, O Lord,” which conclude with: “Alleluia” (3 times), “Glory to Thee, O God.”

After this (instead of “Come, let us give the last kiss”) are sung the Paschal Stichera with verses (“A sacred Pascha”), while the people kiss the Cross. After the stichera the litany for the departed: “Have mercy on us, O God,” with the exclamation. Instead of “Memory eternal,” there is sung: “Christ is risen.”

And when the body is carried unto the cemetery, on the way also is sung: “Christ is risen.” At the cemetery everything is as usual. The Sunday Dismissal.[^303] Instead of “Memory eternal”: “Christ is risen.”[^304]

(p. 263)

§. 191.

The Order of Parastas.[^305]

The presiding Priest and all the Priests present there, having entered into the Sanctuary, clothe themselves in epitrachelion and phelonion, and having gone out from the Sanctuary, stand by the tetrapod, upon which is placed a dish with kolyvo.

The presiding Priest, having taken the censer with incense, censing, beginneth: “Blessed is our God...” etc. (The censing from the 4 sides of the tetrapod taketh place cruciformly.) At this they sing the “Trisagion.” After this: “Our Father,” “Come, let us worship” (3 times), and Psalm 90 (“He that dwelleth in the aid...”); and thus the Litany of Peace.

After the Litany of Peace the cantors sing first “Alleluia” 3 times, and then the troparia: “With depth of wisdom.”

After the troparia are sung the verses from Kathisma 17, and that: first from the 1st stasis: “Blessed are the undefiled,” with the verse: “Blessed art Thou, O Lord, teach us Thy statutes.” After this from the second stasis: “I am Thine,” with the verse: “O Savior, save the soul of Thy servant,” concluding with the sticheron: “My soul shall live.” After this is sung: “The choir of the Saints,” and it concludeth with “Alleluia” (3 times). After this the Little Litany, when the Priest censeth, and the Sessional hymn; and thus followeth Psalm 50.

After this is sung the canon: “Having traversed the water.” After the 3rd ode of the canon taketh place the Sessional hymn, after the 6th the Kontakion and Ikos, and after the 9th “It is truly meet,” when the Priests stand.

After this the Priests sing: “Holy God,” and the Little Panikhida beginneth with the litany for the departed and with the Dismissal. At the conclusion the Priest proclaimeth: “In blessed repose...” etc., and the cantor singeth: “Memory eternal.”

(p. 264)

§. 192.

Instruction concerning the Singing of the Parastas from Thomas Sunday unto the Apodosis of the Feast of the Resurrection.

After “Blessed is our God,” instead of the “Trisagion”: “Christ is risen” 3 times. After this Glory, and Now; Most Holy Trinity; Lord, have mercy (3 times); Our Father, with the exclamation; and everything in order right up unto the canon.

In the canon, for the katavasia are taken the irmosi of the Paschal Canon.

Instead of “It is truly meet”: “The Angel cried.”

Instead of the Trisagion: “Christ is risen” three times. The rest in order; only at the conclusion after the Dismissal: “In blessed repose...”[^306] instead of “Memory eternal”[^307] there is taken: “Christ is risen.”

§. 193.

The Order of the Sanctification of Water on the Feast of Theophany.[^308]

After the Ambo Prayer[^309] the Priest, with candle-bearers preceding him, goeth unto the place where in a certain vessel (in the font) water hath been prepared, while the choir singeth the Troparia: “The voice of the Lord upon the waters,” during which singing the Priest censeth the water. After this follow 3 Paremias [Old Testament readings], the Prokeimenon, the Epistle, the Gospel, and the Litany: “In peace let us pray to the Lord,” with the exclamation.

After this the Priest proclaimeth loudly: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and beginneth the prayer: “O Trinity beyond all essence,” in which prayer, when he cometh unto the words: “Great art Thou, O Lord,” he taketh a three-branched lighted candle and signeth with it cruciformly the water (and that: he speaketh these words 3 times, and 3 times signeth the water); after this he continueth the prayer, and when the Priest speaketh the words: “Do Thou (p. 265)

therefore, O King Who lovest mankind,” he breatheth upon the water cruciformly (and that: inasmuch as he speaketh these words 3 times, therefore also 3 times he breatheth); after this, continuing again, he cometh unto the words: “Do Thou Thyself now also, O Master,” when he blesseth the water cruciformly with his hand, dipping the fingers of his right hand cruciformly (and inasmuch as he speaketh those words 3 times, so also 3 times he dippeth them); and continuing the prayer, he speaketh: “And save, O Lord, Thy servant, our most faithful Emperor...” etc.; these words he proclaimeth loudly 3 times, and the choir answereth 3 times: “Lord, have mercy”; finally the Priest concludeth this prayer: “Of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, now and forever...” etc. After the prayer: “Peace be unto all”; “Bow your heads...” etc., and thus the exclamation, after which the Priest, having finished the prayer, taketh with both hands the holy Cross and immerseth it 3 times into the water, namely: lowering it cruciformly into the water and raising it up, during which is sung the troparion: “When Thou, O Lord, wast baptized in the Jordan...” etc., and that: 3 times.

After this the Priest, having kissed the Cross, sprinkleth the Sanctuary and the whole church, and giveth the Cross unto the people to kiss, and sprinkleth them with holy water, while the choir singeth the troparion: “When Thou, O Lord, wast baptized in the Jordan.” “Glory, and Now”: “Let us the faithful sing praise...” etc. After this taketh place: “Blessed be the name of the Lord,” and thus the Dismissal: “May He Who deigned to be baptized in the Jordan by John for our salvation, Christ our true God...” etc.

§. 194.

The Order of the Sanctification of Water on the Feast of the Procession of the Precious Wood of the Venerable and Life-Giving Cross.[^310]

The Priest, having finished the Holy Liturgy and being vested in phelonion and epitrachelion, after the Ambo Prayer goeth unto the Altar-Table, while the cantor singeth: “God is the Lord and hath appeared unto us” (3 times) etc., with the troparion unto the Mother of God, “Glory, and Now”: “We shall never be silent.” Psalm 50, after which the Priest singeth troparia in honor of the Mother of God: “Having received joy from the Angel,” while the cantor answereth: “Most Holy Mother of God, save us.” After “Glory, and Now,” the Priest singeth again troparia unto the Most Holy Mother of God, the Archangels, the Forerunner, the Apostles, the Unmercen- (p. 266)

-aries, with “Glory, and Now.” And the cantor answereth: “Most Holy Lady, pray to God for us”; “Holy Apostles, pray to God for us”; “Holy Forerunner, pray to God for us,” etc. After these troparia the cantors begin to sing the troparia: “The doors of compassion,” and immediately follow the idiomela troparia: “Now the time hath come,” after which the Priest proclaimeth: “For holy art Thou,” the Trisagion, the Prokeimenon, the Epistle, and the Gospel. After this the Litany of Peace and the prayer: “O Lord our God...” etc., in which when the Priest speaketh these words: “And send down the grace of Thy Holy Spirit,” he blesseth the water cruciformly, dipping his fingers into it 3 times; in the continuation of this prayer he speaketh 3 times the words: “Preserve Thy servant, our most faithful Emperor,” unto which the choir singeth: “Lord, have mercy.” After the prayer, which endeth with “now and forever, and unto the ages of ages,” followeth: “Peace be unto all”; “Bow your heads...” etc., with the exclamation. — After the exclamation the Priest blesseth the water cruciformly, immersing the Cross into the water 3 times, lowering and raising it up, and singeth the troparion: “Save, O Lord...” etc., which the choir also repeateth 2 times. After this the Priest — having kissed the Cross and giving it unto the people to kiss — sprinkleth all present with holy water, likewise the Sanctuary and the whole church. The cantor singeth the stichera: “Fountain of healings.” After which followeth: “Blessed be the name of the Lord,” and the Dismissal of the Liturgy.

§. 195.

The Order of the Sanctification of Water in Brief.

This sanctification is not celebrated publicly in church, but only privately (apart) in case of necessity for the sake of speed. — In church publicly only those two sanctifications of water are celebrated concerning which mention hath been made. — This brief sanctification, however, is celebrated thus:

The Priest, having taken upon himself the epitrachelion, beginneth: “Blessed is our God.” The cantor readeth: Heavenly King; Trisagion; Our Father; Lord, have mercy (12 times); Come, let us worship (3 times). And immediately Psalm 28: “Bring unto the Lord, O ye sons of God...” etc.

After the Psalm: Glory, and Now; Alleluia (3 times), and there is taken the troparion: “Send down grace from heaven...” etc.

After this the Priest proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and readeth over the water prepared in a vessel the prayer: “O God of great name...” etc.

After the prayer he immerseth the Cross or the fingers of his right hand (p. 267)

three times cruciformly into the water, lowering and raising it, and singing three times the troparion: “Save, O Lord, Thy people.”

After this he sprinkleth with the holy water all those present, and maketh the dismissal of the day.

§. 196.

Order of the Blessing of Palms.

The Blessing of Palms taketh place on Palm Sunday at Matins. The cantor after the Gospel beginneth to read Psalm 50, and the Priest, without saying “Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ,” immediately goeth forth unto the branches and censeth them cruciformly. When the choir hath finished Psalm 50, the Priest proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and in a loud voice saith the prayer: “O Lord our God, Who sittest upon the Cherubim,” etc. After this: “Peace be unto all.” “Bow your heads,” etc., and the exclamation: “O Lord God Almighty,” etc. At the conclusion of this the Priest sprinkleth the branches with holy water, saying: “These branches and palms are blessed and sanctified by the sprinkling of this holy water, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, Amen,” etc. At the conclusion of this the Priest giveth the Gospel to kiss and distributeth the branches; and Matins continueth as prescribed.

§. 197.

Order of the Blessing of Foods.

The Blessing of Foods taketh place on Pascha Sunday.

At the Liturgy after the Ambo Prayer and after “Christ is risen” hath been sung three times, etc., the Priest proclaimeth: “Glory to the Holy, Consubstantial,” etc., unto which the choir singeth: “Amen,” etc. After this “Christ is risen” three times, during which singing the Priest, having come unto the place where the artos and the foods have been prepared, censeth them. After this he proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and reciteth three prayers, namely: a) for the blessing of the artos, b) for the blessing of the lamb and other meat foods, and c) for the blessing of dairy and other foods. And having finished these prayers, he sprinkleth the foods with holy water, saying: “These foods are blessed and sanctified by the sprinkling of this holy water, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit,” etc., and the Dismissal.

(p. 268)

§. 198.

Order of the Blessing of a Sown Field.

The blessing of a sown field taketh place around the feast of Saint Mark, and that in the following manner:

When the Priest at the Liturgy after “Blessed be the name of the Lord” hath pronounced the words: “Let us depart in peace,” etc., the procession, having gone forth from the church, with the cross-bearer preceding, goeth unto the field. On the way various songs are sung, especially: “Our Father” and “O Theotokos Virgin.”[^311]

Having arrived at the field at the designated place, the Priest first of all turneth with his face toward the east (First Stasis), and the cantor beginneth to read Psalm 84: “Lord, Thou hast been favorable unto Thy land.” After this: “Glory, and Now. Alleluia” (three times), and the Priest proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and in a loud voice reciteth the prayer: “O Lord God from before all ages,” etc., and so follow the Gospel and the second prayer: “O Almighty God.” After this the Priest first conjureth with the words: “I conjure you, aerial waves,” etc., afterwards sprinkleth the field with holy water and blesseth the seeds, saying: “May God bless,” etc., and with this he concludeth the stasis.

After this the Priest turneth toward the south (Second Stasis), and the choir readeth Psalm 125: “When the Lord turned again the captivity of Zion”; after this the Priest in a loud voice reciteth the prayer: “O Holy Master,” then the Gospel, and again saith the prayer: “Multiply, we pray Thee, O Master”; after the prayer he first conjureth, saying: “I conjure you, clouds,” etc., and so blesseth the field with holy water, saying: “Bless, O Lord, the earth,” etc.

At the Third Stasis the Priest turneth toward the west; the cantor readeth Psalm 146: “Praise the Lord”; after which the Priest in a loud voice reciteth the prayer: “O Master Jesus Christ,” readeth the Gospel, and again the second prayer: “O Almighty,” etc., after which he first conjureth with the words: “I conjure you, waves and winds,” etc., and only so blesseth, saying: “May He bless you, the seeds,” etc.

At the Fourth Stasis he turneth toward the north, and the choir readeth Psalm 147: “Praise Jerusalem,” after which the Priest in a loud voice reciteth the prayer: “O Creator of all,” readeth the Gospel, and the second prayer in a loud voice: “O Holy Master,” etc. And he conjureth with the words: “I conjure you, devils,” etc.; afterwards he blesseth the field, sprinkling with holy water, saying as before: “May He bless,” and with this is concluded also the Fourth Stasis, after which they return unto the church.

(p. 269)

Here is pronounced the litany: “Have mercy on us, O God,” etc. (as at the Midnight Office), and thus the usual dismissal.

§. 199.

Order of the Blessing of Herbs.

Among us for the most part herbs are blessed on the day of the Nativity of Saint John the Baptist, in some places however on the Dormition of the Most Holy Virgin.

After the Liturgy after “Blessed be the name,” the Priest proclaimeth: “Blessed is our God,” and beginneth to cense the herbs, which the people hold standing on both sides from the Royal Doors even unto the church gates. The cantor, having sung “Amen,” saith: “Heavenly King,” “Trisagion,” “Our Father,” “Lord, have mercy” (12 times), “Come, let us worship” (three times), and immediately readeth Psalm 64 (“Praise is meet for Thee”). After the Psalm he singeth the troparion and kontakion of the feast, unto which the Priest: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and in a loud voice reciteth the prayer: “O Almighty and Pre-Eternal God.” After this prayer he in a loud voice reciteth the second prayer: “O God, Who through Moses,” and so: “Peace be unto all,” “Bow your heads,” etc., with the exclamation. After the exclamation he sprinkleth the herbs with holy water, walking in the midst of the people and saying: “May the blessing of God Almighty, the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit, descend upon this creation and abide with it always. Amen,” etc. Dismissal.

§. 200.

What Other Blessings We Have.

a) The Blessing of Candles: it is twofold: on the feast of the Meeting of the Lord before the Holy Liturgy solemnly;[^312] on other feast days, however, it is performed in brief, and that in such a manner, that after “Blessed be the name of the Lord” the Priest proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and readeth the prayer: “O God Almighty,” etc. After the prayer he sprinkleth the candles with holy water, saying: “These lights are sanctified by the sprinkling of this holy water, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, Amen.”

b) Grapes or vine berries and grain seeds are blessed on the feast of the Transfiguration of Christ, and that in token of thanksgiving for the receiving of these fruits and in test- (p. 270)

-imony that the wine and bread from these fruits must be brought for the sacred action.

c) On the feast of the Nativity of the Most Holy Virgin the field seeds that have already been sown are blessed.

d) On the day of the Falling Asleep of Saint John the Theologian taketh place the blessing of wine.

Besides these, yet other blessings of this kind are traced in the Trebnik, but concerning these we do not speak.

§. 201.

Order of the Blessing and Laying of the First Stone for the Foundation of a Church.

The Priest, having received from the Bishop the authority for the foundation of a church and for the blessing of the first stone, performeth this rite in this manner: On the preceding day the Priest erecteth a wooden cross upon that place where the Altar-Table is to be; and by the workmen on that place where the foundation is to be, trenches are excavated, and stones are prepared, of which one four-cornered stone is to be with the representation of the Holy Cross.

On the following day, on the day of the blessing, the Priest vesteth in bright vestments and goeth with the whole clergy, the deacons preceding him with censers, and the church servers with holy water and with other necessary things, unto this place. As they go, the cantors sing the litiya stichera of the Saint unto whom the temple will be dedicated.

Having arrived at the place, they place upon the prepared Tetrapod candles, the Gospel, and the Holy Cross.[^313] And the Priest, having censed the Tetrapod cruciformly, proclaimeth: “Blessed is our God,” etc. After this followeth: “Heavenly King,” “Trisagion,” “Our Father,” “Come, let us worship” (three times), etc. Psalm 83: (“How lovely are Thy tabernacles, O Lord.”) The Litany of Peace and the troparia: “Thou Who hast founded it, O Christ,” etc.

After the troparia the Priest sprinkleth with holy water the place where the Holy Cross is to be — or hath already been erected — and saith the prayer: “Deign, O Christ our God,” etc.; and having taken the Holy Cross, if it is not yet erected, he erecteth it, while the choir singeth the troparion: “The Cross having been erected on the earth.” Afterwards the Priest (p. 271)

before the erected cross readeth this prayer: “O Lord, Incomprehensible God,” etc.

After the prayer follow the Prokeimenon, the Epistle, the Gospel.

After this the Priest goeth unto the trench where the foundation is to be laid, and with all the clergy singeth in Tone 6: “Arise, as tomorrow,” etc., and there is first read Psalm 126: “Except the Lord build the house.” After this the Priest in a loud voice reciteth the prayers: “O Lord Jesus Christ,” and “Bless, O Lord, this creation.”

After the prayers the Priest sprinkleth the stone with holy water and with his own hands layeth it upon the foundation of the sanctuary, saying: “In the faith of Jesus Christ, this first stone, being laid into this foundation, I bless and establish: In the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, Amen,” etc.

And the stone laid and strengthened he sprinkleth with holy water, saying: “How awesome is this place,” etc.[^314]

After this the choir readeth Psalm 86: “His foundation is in the holy mountains,” at the conclusion of which the Priest encompasseth the whole place of the sanctuary, sprinkling it with holy water, going first toward the north, and after that toward the west.

Finally, having returned unto the place where he had begun to sprinkle, he standeth before the erected cross, saying: “Again and again, having bowed our knees, let us pray to the Lord,” and there follow two prayers: “O God Almighty,” and “O Lord our God.” At the end, when they have stood up, the Litany: “Have mercy on us, O God,” the Dismissal, and Many Years.

§. 202.

Order of the Blessing of a New Church.[^315]

The Priest, having permission from the Bishop, wishing to bless a new church, after the singing of Matins, vested in all the priestly vestments, goeth with the other invited Priests unto the doors of the church, one holy cross preceding them, where first of all he saith secretly the prayer of intention: “O God of our action.” After this he beginneth in a loud voice: “Blessed is our God,” etc. “Heavenly King,” “Trisagion,” “Our Father,” “Come, let us worship” (three times). Psalm 50.

(p. 272)

During the reading of the Psalm the Priest, compassing round about from without the church, empty and stripped of all sanctuary adornments, sprinkleth its walls with holy water, saying: “Thou shalt sprinkle me with hyssop,” etc. The cantor, having finished Psalm 50, singeth the troparia: “The circuit of the sanctification today,” etc.

After the sprinkling from without, the Priest, standing before the church doors, proclaimeth in a loud voice: “With compunction, having bowed our knees, let us pray to the Lord,” and the Priest, having bowed his knees, saith the prayer: “O Lord God of mercy,” etc.

After this prayer the Priest, having arisen, signeth the church doors cruciformly with his right hand, saying: “In the name of the Father, † and of the Son, † and of the Holy Spirit, † with peace let us enter.” The choir singeth unto this: “In the name of the Lord,” etc., and the Priest, having opened the doors with the holy cross, first entereth himself into the church, and after him the others, singing the sticheron: “Even as the house of Zacchaeus,” etc.

After the entry into the church the Priest standeth before the Royal Doors, and the choir readeth Psalm 121: (“I was glad of them that said unto me”), after which the Priest, having chanted the litany: “In peace let us pray to the Lord,” proclaimeth: “With compunction, all having bowed the knees, let us pray to the Lord,” and having bowed his knees, he readeth in a loud voice the prayer: “O God, Almighty, All-Bountiful,” etc. After the prayer the cantor readeth Psalm 120, during the reading of which the Priest, having arisen, sprinkleth with holy water the Altar-Table, the Table of Oblation, and the right and left walls of the church, saying: “Thou shalt sprinkle me with hyssop, and I shall be cleansed.” The choir, having read the Psalm, singeth beneath this the sticheron in Tone 6: “Bless, O Lord, this temple,” and again readeth Psalm 25: “Judge me, O Lord,” etc.

After the reading of the Psalm (if there be not the Liturgy) follow: the Prokeimenon, the Epistle, the Gospel (of the sanctification of a church), and the sticheron: “O Lord, the ranks and hosts of Angels in the heavens,” etc.[^316]

And the Priest again before the great Throne proclaimeth: “Again and again, with compunction having bowed the knees, let us pray to the Lord,” and having bowed the knees, he saith the prayers: “O Lord God of hosts,” and “O God and Father Almighty,” etc.

After this the Priest sanctifieth — if they be present — the new priestly vestments, and having vested in them, and having placed the Antimension, the Epistle, the Gospel, and the Holy Cross upon the holy Altar-Table, beginneth the Divine Litur- (p. 273)

-gy. — After the Gospel, however, or after the Ambo Prayer he readeth the prayer: “Unoriginate, Unchangeable,” etc.

At the end is sung the thanksgiving hymn of Saint Ambrose: “We praise Thee, O God,” and thus the dismissal and Many Years.

§. 203.

Order of the Blessing of the Altar-Table Itself.

The Altar-Table is twofold: one immovable, and the other movable, that is: the Antimension. Both are consecrated by the Bishop, but for the blessing of an immovable Altar-Table a Priest also can have authority from the Bishop.

The Priest, wishing to bless an Altar-Table, vesteth in all the priestly vestments and, having come before the Altar-Table that is to be blessed, standeth there before the placed Tetrapod. Upon this Tetrapod are placed the Cross, lighted candles, holy water, a sprinkler, and likewise also the Antimension, which must be placed upon the new Altar-Table.

After this he performeth this rite thus: First, standing before the Tetrapod, he proclaimeth: “Blessed is our God,” etc. “Heavenly King,” etc., “Trisagion,” etc., “Our Father.” “Come, let us worship” (three times). Psalm 83: (“How lovely are Thy dwellings, O Lord,” etc.) During the Psalm the Priest sprinkleth the Altar-Table with holy water, saying: “Thou shalt sprinkle me with hyssop, and I shall be cleansed; Thou shalt wash me, and I shall be made whiter than snow.” The cantor, after the Psalm, singeth the troparion of the Saint whose image is above the Altar-Table.

The Priest, after the sprinkling, placeth the Antimension upon the Altar-Table, and censeth the table. After this he proclaimeth: “With compunction, having bowed our knees, let us pray to the Lord,” and the Priest, having bowed his knees, in a loud voice readeth the prayer: “O Lord God of hosts,” etc. At the end the usual dismissal of the day, and the Divine Liturgy is celebrated upon the newly-blessed Altar-Table.

§. 204.

Order of the Blessing of Altar Linens.

The linens are placed upon the Tetrapod before the Royal Doors. The Priest, vested in epitrachelion and phelonion, goeth forth from the Sanctuary and, having censed them cruciformly, proclaimeth in a loud voice: “Blessed is our God.” After this: Heavenly King. Trisagion. Our Father. Lord, have mercy (12 times). Come, let us worship (three times). And Psalm 92: (“The Lord reigneth.”)

(p. 274)

After the Psalm the Priest proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and readeth the prayer: “O Lord God, Father Almighty,” etc. Unto this: “Peace be unto all.” “Bow your heads unto the Lord,” and the Exclamation.

After the Exclamation the Priest sprinkleth the coverings, saying: “These coverings are sanctified by the grace of the All-Holy Spirit, by the sprinkling of this holy water, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, Amen.”

And having made the usual Dismissal, he taketh them himself and covereth with them the Altar-Table and the Table of Oblation.

§. 205.

Order of the Blessing and Sanctification of Sacred Vessels, that is: the Diskos, Chalice, Star, and Spoon.

To bless and sanctify the Chalice, Diskos, Star, Spoon, and Ciborium pertaineth properly unto the Bishop alone; but if a Priest receiveth authority for this from the Bishop, he performeth this in this manner:

Before the Royal Doors a Tetrapod is placed, and upon it the vessels. The Priest, vested in epitrachelion and phelonion, cometh hither and first censeth them cruciformly, and after this proclaimeth: “Blessed is our God.” Heavenly King. Trisagion. Our Father. Lord, have mercy (12 times). Come, let us worship (12 times). And thus is read Psalm 22: “The Lord is my shepherd.”

After the Psalm the Priest proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and in a loud voice reciteth the prayer: “O Master Almighty,” etc.

After this prayer he first sanctifieth the chalice and cup, proclaiming: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and saying the prayer: “O Master Almighty, unto Thee we pray,” etc., and immediately: “Peace be unto all.” “Bow your heads,” and the exclamation: “For Thou art the fountain,” etc. And upon this he sprinkleth the vessels with holy water, saying: “These vessels are sanctified by the grace of the All-Holy Spirit, by the sprinkling of this holy water, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, Amen.”

After this, wishing to sanctify the Star, he again proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and readeth the prayer: “O Lord Jesus Christ our God,” etc. After the prayer he sprinkleth the Star with holy water, saying: “This Star is sanctified by the grace of the All-Holy Spirit, by the sprinkling of this holy water: in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, Amen.”

Finally he sanctifieth the Spoon, proclaiming: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and readeth the prayer: “O Lord God Almighty, with the coal taken with tongs by the Seraph,” etc. Then he sprinkleth the Spoon with holy water, saying: “This Spoon is sanctified by the grace (p. 275)

of the All-Holy Spirit, by the sprinkling of this holy water, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, Amen,” and thus he maketh the dismissal of the day.

Note: The blessing and sanctification of the Ciborium, that is, the Tabernacle, is performed in the likeness of the blessing and sanctification of the Chalice.

§. 206.

Order of the Blessing of New Priestly Vestments.[^317]

Before the Royal Doors upon the prepared Tetrapod are placed the new vestments brought for blessing. The Priest, vested in epitrachelion and phelonion, first censeth them, saying as usual: “Blessed is our God,” etc. After this: “Amen.” “Heavenly King,” etc. Trisagion. Our Father. Lord, have mercy (12 times). “Come, let us worship” (three times). And Psalm 92. After the Psalm the Priest proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and readeth the prayer: “O Lord God Almighty,” etc. After this: “Peace be unto all.” “Bow your heads unto the Lord,” and the exclamation. After the exclamation the Priest sprinkleth the vestments set forth with holy water, saying: “These vestments are sanctified (he nameth them) by the grace of the All-Holy Spirit, by the sprinkling of this holy water, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit,” etc. And the dismissal of the day and Many Years.

§. 207.

Order of the Blessing of Church Banners.

The Priest, vested in epitrachelion and phelonion, having censed the banners, beginneth: “Blessed is our God,” etc., unto which the choir answereth: “Amen,” and saith: “Trisagion,” and “Our Father.” After the exclamation the Priest saith: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and in a loud voice reciteth the prayer: “O Eternal God without beginning.” And he sprinkleth the banners with holy water, saying: “These banners are sanctified by the grace of the All-Holy Spirit, by the sprinkling of this holy water: in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, Amen,” and the Dismissal.

§. 208.

Order of the Blessing of the Iconostasis.

An Iconostasis can be erected either in a blessed church or in an unblessed church.

(p. 276)

a) If the Iconostasis is erected in a blessed church, then it is blessed in this manner: The Priest, vested in epitrachelion and phelonion, beginneth before the Royal Doors, saying: “Blessed is our God.” Heavenly King. Trisagion. Our Father. Come, let us worship (three times). And immediately the cantor readeth Psalm 131: (“Lord, remember David”), during which the Priest censeth, and after this, having said “Let us pray to the Lord,” in a loud voice reciteth the prayer: “O Lord our God, God of gods,” etc., after the prayer: “Peace be unto all,” “Bow your heads,” and the exclamation: “For Thou art the God of mercy.” After this he sprinkleth with holy water the whole Iconostasis from the bottom even unto the top, saying: “These Icons are sanctified by the sprinkling of this holy water in honor and glory of the great God and our Savior Jesus Christ, and in memory of His Saints: in the name of the Father, † and of the Son, † and of the Holy Spirit, † Amen.” — After this the cantor readeth the troparia: “Unto the Most Pure Image.” Glory: “Apostles, Prophets,” etc. And Now: “With love, O Pure Virgin,” etc. And thus he maketh the usual dismissal.

b) But if the Iconostasis is erected in an unblessed church, and it is to be blessed by a Priest, then first the church is blessed, and so before the dismissal the Iconostasis is blessed. That is: the Priest, having stood before the Royal Doors, after he hath first said: “Let us pray to the Lord,” readeth the prayer: “O Lord our God, God of gods,” etc., and after the prayer continueth as was said above, and only thus maketh the dismissal.

§. 209.

Order of the Blessing of Bells (Cymbals).

The bell that is to be blessed is suspended in a prepared place, and that in such a manner that it may be sprinkled both from without and from within. The Priest, having taken upon himself the epitrachelion and phelonion, beginneth: “Glory to the Holy,” etc. And immediately: “Trisagion,” and “Our Father.” After the exclamation the choir singeth the troparion: “Blessed art Thou, O Christ our God,” with the kontakion: “When He came down and confused the tongues.”

After this the Priest with his hand blesseth the bell three times, saying: “In the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit,” etc., and in a loud voice proclaimeth the Litany: “In peace let us pray to the Lord.”

After the Litany the Priest giveth a name unto the bell, saying: “In the name of the Lord Jesus Christ, let this bell be named (N.), according to the grace given unto us from the Life-Creating Spirit, Amen,” and having taken the censer, he censeth the bell from four sides from without and from within. The choir unto this readeth Psalm 28.

(p. 277)

After the Psalm the Priest proclaimeth in a loud voice: “Again and again, bending our knees, let us pray to the Lord.” And having bent his knees and laid his hand upon the bell, he proclaimeth in a loud voice the prayer: “O Lord our God,” etc. After this the Priest, having stood up, sprinkleth the bell with holy water on four sides on the exterior and on four sides from within, saying: “This bell is blessed and sanctified,” etc., and having lifted up his hands he saith: “Glory to Thee, O Holy Trinity, our God, glory to Thee!”
After this he anointeth the bell with holy myron, saying: “Unto the glory of the Holy Trinity. Alleluia” (three times). — The choir unto this readeth Psalm 142, and the Priest after the Psalm proclaimeth in a loud voice the prayer: “O God, the Only One,” etc., and immediately maketh the Dismissal.

§. 210.

Order of the Blessing of a Bell Tower.

The Priest — having received authority for this blessing — cometh unto the newly dedicated bell tower and standeth on the western side facing the east. Before the Priest standeth a Tetrapod, upon which are placed: burning candles, the Cross, the Gospel, and in a vessel holy water with a sprinkler. — Having taken upon himself the epitrachelion and phelonion, he censeth round about the bell tower and beginneth the rite: “Blessed is our God,” etc. (“Heavenly King,” “Trisagion,” “Come, let us worship,” Psalm 60). After the Psalm is sung: the troparion of the church with its kontakion.
After this the Priest proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and in a loud voice readeth the prayer: “O Lord our God,” etc.; after the prayer he sprinkleth round about the bell tower, saying: “By the sprinkling of this holy water this tower is sanctified unto the glory of the Lord,” etc. After this he standeth before the tower, looking toward the east, and beginneth: “And that we may be accounted worthy,” etc., and readeth the Gospel, after which followeth the Litany: “Have mercy on us, O God,” and the Dismissal.

§. 211.

Order of the Blessing of a Cross to be Erected upon the Roof of a Newly Built Church.

The Priest in epitrachelion censeth the cross and beginneth as usual: “Blessed is our God,” etc., “Trisagion,” “Our Father,” etc. And immediately the troparia are sung: “Save, O Lord, Thy people,” “Glory”: Kontakion: “Thou Who wast lifted up upon the Cross of Thine own will,” “Both now”: “Protection of Christians.” After this the Priest, having said “Let us pray to the Lord,” proclaimeth in a loud voice the prayer: “O Lord Almighty,” after which (p. 278)

he sprinkleth the holy cross with holy water, saying: “This sign of the Cross is blessed and sanctified by the grace of the Holy Spirit, and by the sprinkling of this holy water,” etc. Unto this is sung the kontakion (“Thou Who wast lifted up”), and the Priest maketh the usual dismissal, while the workmen erect the holy cross upon the top of the church.

§. 212.

Order of the Blessing of a Cemetery.

On the day preceding the blessing of the cemetery the Priest erecteth upon it a tall wooden cross, according to the height of a man, and beside that same cross he erecteth a single wooden candlestick for the placement of three candles.
On the following day in the morning the Priest, vested in priestly vestments, with other priests, preceded by the cross-bearer, goeth unto the cross erected in the cemetery, carrying with them the Trebnik, three candles, holy water, and a censer. Having arrived at the place of the erected cross and the candles having been lit and set upon the three-branched candlestick, he beginneth: “Blessed is our God,” etc. The choir unto this saith: “Amen,” and “Heavenly King,” “Trisagion,” “Our Father,” “Come, let us worship” (three times), Psalm 50.
After the Psalm the Priest intoneth the Litany of Peace, and after this he sprinkleth the erected cross and the entire place of the cemetery, saying: “Thou shalt sprinkle me with hyssop, and I shall be cleansed; Thou shalt wash me, and I shall be made whiter than snow.” And during this the choir singeth the troparia: “Thee we implore,” etc., after which the Priest proclaimeth in a loud voice the prayer: “O God Almighty.”
And immediately followeth: the Prokimenon, the Apostle, and the Gospel. After this the Priest saith the prayer: “O God of all the world,” and “Peace be unto all,” “Bow your heads unto the Lord,” etc., and the exclamation: “For Thou art the Resurrection.” After this the Priest, of the three lighted candles, affixeth one upon the top, and the other two upon the arms of the cross, and censeth and sprinkleth the Holy Cross. — At the end there is a litany for the departed (“Have mercy on us, O God”) and thus the Dismissal.

§. 213.

Order of the Blessing and Sanctification of a Newly Erected Cross.

The Priest, vested in epitrachelion and phelonion, having come unto the cross beginneth: “Blessed is our God,” unto which the choir singeth: “Amen,” and there followeth: “Trisagion,” “Our Father,” “Come, let us worship” (three times).

(p. 279)

Psalm 98, and the cantors sing the troparia: “Save, O Lord, Thy people,” “Glory”: Kontakion: “Thou Who wast lifted up,” “Both now”: “The Life-Creating Cross.”
After this all sing three times the troparion: “Send down Thy grace.”
After the singing of the troparia the Priest proclaimeth in a loud voice the prayer: “O Lord God,” and “Peace be unto all,” “Bow your heads,” etc., and the exclamation: “For Thou art a God of mercy and compassions,” etc., and then he sprinkleth the cross with holy water, saying: “This sign of the Cross is sanctified,” etc., and censeth it crosswise unto the stichera: “The Cross is the guardian of the whole inhabited earth.”
At the end is sung: “We bow before Thy Cross,” etc., three times, and then while the choir singeth “Come, ye faithful,” first the Priest, and then the rest bow down and kiss the holy cross, after which followeth the Dismissal.

§. 214.

Order of the Blessing of a New House.[^318]

The Priest coming into the house that is to be blessed bringeth holy water with him in a vessel, likewise also oil blessed at the All-Night Vigil in a small vessel. The house that is to be blessed must be prepared in this manner: that upon the four walls the sign of the cross be drawn, and in the midst of the house a Tetrapod covered with an embroidered cloth, and upon it are placed the holy cross and burning candles.
The Priest, having come into the house, having taken upon himself the epitrachelion and phelonion, beginneth as usual: “Blessed is our God,” etc., “Heavenly King,” “Trisagion,” “Our Father,” “Lord, have mercy” (12 times), “Come, let us worship” (three times), etc. And Psalms 126, 127, and 132 are read. After the Psalms the troparion: “As unto the house of Zacchaeus,” etc., unto which the Priest proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and in a loud voice readeth the prayers: “To Thee we fervently pray,” etc., and “Visit, we pray Thee,” etc.
After these prayers the Priest first sprinkleth the walls of the house with holy water, saying first: a) “In the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, Amen”; b) after this: “By this sprinkling of holy water may the Lord grant unto this house and unto the things found therein His blessing and a peaceful dwelling”; c) at the end: “May the blessing of God the Almighty, the Father, † and the Son, and the Holy Spirit, † descend upon this place and abide forever. Amen.”

(p. 280)

After this, secondly, he anointeth with oil the walls of the house, having drawn the sign of the cross, and that in the center of the drawn cross, beginning from the eastern wall, then the western, northern, and finally the southern, at each anointing saying: “This house is blessed by the anointing of this holy oil in the name of the Father, † and of the Son, † and of the Holy Spirit, † Amen.”[^319] — After this anointing he lighteth the candles prepared before each sign of the cross. The choir during this sprinkling and during this anointing singeth the sticheron: “Bless, O Lord, this house.” When the anointing is completed, the Priest, standing toward the east, readeth Psalm 100, and during this the Priest censeth the whole house, and immediately singeth the Litany: “Have mercy on us, O God,” after the Litany maketh the Dismissal, and proclaimeth Many Years.

§. 215.

What the Priest Performeth on the First Day of the Holy Great Fast.[^320]

On this day (that is, on the First Monday of the Great Fast), after the divine service of the day, the Priest instructeth his spiritual children how they ought to prepare themselves through the holy Fast for the Passions of Christ. And having instructed them sufficiently, he biddeth them bend their knees before the Lord and proclaimeth in a loud voice the prayer: “O Lord Jesus Christ our God, the Hope of all the ends of the earth,” etc. And after the prayer he blesseth them with his right hand, saying: “The blessing of the Lord be upon you, by His grace and love for mankind, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages.”

§. 216.

What is the Dread Prayer?

The dread prayer is a service over those who suffer from demons. It is the adjuration of the devil. It consisteth of Psalms 142, 22, 26, 64, and 50, further of the canon to Jesus Christ, to the Most Pure Virgin, to the Bodiless Powers, to the Apostles, and to All Saints, and of the exorcistic prayers of Saint Basil the Great and Saint John Chrysostom.[^321]

(p. 281)

It is the most important exorcism. This service is not permitted to be celebrated without permission obtained for each individual case from the Bishop.
Besides this exorcism, there is in the Church also: $\alpha$) the adjuration of the devil over the Catechumen before Holy Baptism; and $\beta$) over the water at the blessing of the same; $\gamma$) there is a prayer over a dwelling disturbed by evil spirits; and $\delta$) there is a prayer for the driving away of insects and other noxious vermin harmful to fields and vineyards.

§. 217.

Order of the Akathist to the Dormition of the Most Holy Virgin Mary.[^322]

The Priest, in epitrachelion, standing before the Royal Doors, proclaimeth: “Blessed is our God.” The choir: “Amen,” and immediately “Heavenly King,” “Trisagion,” “Our Father,” and the exclamation. After the exclamation the Priest entereth into the Sanctuary, and the cantors sing the troparia: “Have mercy on us, O Lord,” etc. “Glory”: “Lord, have mercy on us,” “Both now”: “Open unto us the doors of compassion.” After this: Lord, have mercy (12 times), “Glory, Both now,” “Come, let us worship” (three times), and immediately Psalm 142: “Lord, hear.” After the Psalm, “Glory, Both now,” and the troparion: “In giving birth, O Virgin.” After the troparion: Lord, have mercy (three times), “Glory, Both now,” and Psalm 50: “Have mercy on me, O God.”
After this the Priest cometh forth, and before the Tetrapod, vested in phelonion, singeth alternately 13 kontakia and 13 ikoi. (The kontakia are: “To the chosen from all generations,” “Seeing the Apostles,” “The power of the Most High,” “Having within a storm,” “The God-guided stars,” “The God-bearing heralds, the Apostles,” “Desiring all,” “Seeing Thee strange in the highest,” “All nature,” “Desiring to save the world,” “All singing,” “The grace of high-enthroned glory,” and “O all-hymned Mother.” — And the ikoi are: “The foremost Angel,” “All rational creation,” “The disciple having within a thought,” “The initiates having heard,” “The God-beholders seeing their Master,” “Unto us the Sun of righteousness hath shone,” “Showing a new wonder,” “All being on high,” “The oratory of the God-enlightened,” “A rampart art Thou unto the faithful,” “The light-receiving lamp,” “Singing Thine all-venerable dormition,” and “The foremost Angel.”[^323])

(p. 282)

At the end the Priest again singeth once more the kontakion: “To the chosen from all generations,” etc.
Unto this the cantors sing the Exaposteilarion of the feast three times: “Ye Apostles from the ends of the earth.”
After the Exaposteilarion, “Lord, have mercy” (three times), and immediately the Priest, standing before the Tetrapod, proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and kneeling, readeth two prayers. The first: “O Most Holy Theotokos, Virgin Lady,” etc. After the first prayer he again proclaimeth: “Let us pray to the Lord,” and only then readeth the second prayer: “O my Most Gracious Queen,” etc.
After the prayers the cantors sing: “It is truly proper”; and immediately “Trisagion,” “Our Father,” the troparion: “In giving birth, virginity,” the Litany: “Have mercy on us, O God.” “Again we pray that this city may be preserved,” etc., the exclamation: “Hear us, O God,” etc. “Glory to Thee, O Christ God.” The Dismissal of the Dormition: “The blessing of the Lord be upon you,” etc.[^324]

## APPENDIX

§. 218.

When We Use the Censer.[^325]

We use the censer in our divine services many times, namely:
1. At Great Vespers: a) At “Lord, I have cried” (in epitrachelion).[^326] b) At the Great Entrance. c) Before the beginning of the All-Night Vigil in phelonion and with open doors. d) At the Litiya according to custom, for the Typikon itself doth not prescribe censing the Holy Table, nor the Iconostasis, but only the vestibule and the people there. e) At the blessing of loaves, only the offered gifts from four sides. f) At Great Compline on the Nativity of Christ, Theophany, and the Annunciation, before the beginning, with open Royal Doors — in phelonion.
Note: At Small Vespers only once, at “Lord, I have cried” (but this is omitted among us).
2. At Great Matins: At the beginning, when there is a vigil, we do not cense; but we cense: a) At the beginning of Matins in (p. 283)

epitrachelion at the time of the very beginning: “Blessed is our God,” without opening the Royal Doors, but going forth through the northern doors — we cense the whole church and the vestibule (but among us this doth not take place). b) At the time of singing: “Let every breath praise the Lord” before the Gospel. c) After the 8th Ode, when they sing: “My soul doth magnify the Lord” or the refrains of the feast.[^327]
Note: At weekday Matins it is prescribed to cense the Sanctuary and the whole church only at the very beginning and at the 9th Ode in epitrachelion with closed Royal Doors (but that doth not take place among us).
3. At the Divine Liturgies: a) At the Proskomide we cense each veil and the gifts already covered, with the prayer: “Blessed is our God Who art thus well-pleased.” b) Before the beginning of the Liturgy. c) At the time of the reading of the Apostle, the Table of Oblation, the entire Sanctuary, and from the Royal Doors the people are censed. d) At the Cherubic Hymn. e) After the consecration of the Holy Gifts at “Especially.” f) At the end, if there be an offering, that is, *kolyvo*, then before the prayer we cense it. g) If at the Liturgy for the departed there is also a Panakhyda, then we cense at “With the spirits of the righteous” the whole church (excepting within the Sanctuary); further in the Litany for the departed at each petition three times the Sanctuary; and at “In blessed dormition” likewise.
4. At funerals and parastases censing taketh place: a) at the beginning; b) after each litany for the departed; and c) at “With the spirits of the righteous.”
5. Further, we use the censer also at Great Blessings of Water, at the beginning or before the Gospel, and besides this also at many church sanctifications and blessings, but for this see the Trebnik.

§. 219.

What is Necessary to Note Concerning the Color of Church Vestments?

In the Western-Latin Church the color of church vestments is strictly regulated, and it is fivefold: white, red [crimson], green, violet, and black;[^328] further, vestments of diverse colors may be used freely only if one of these five colors is the fundamental color; moreover, it is most precisely (p. 284)

determined also which color of vestments it behoveth to use on individual days and at various divine services.
Among us in the Eastern-Greek Church, however, regarding the color of church vestments there is no precise arrangement. The ecclesiastical books speak of bright, red [crimson], and black vestments.
Bright vestments[^329] are used on festive days, red [crimson] on fasting days, and black on days of commemoration for the departed.[^330]
Furthermore, the color of vestments hath its symbolic significance: for white vestments are a symbol of innocence, sanctity, and joy; red vestments are a symbol of sorrow and repentance;[^331] while black vestments are a sign of grief. And on this account it is not fitting to use vestments of diverse color, or of such color as is not accepted by the Church or doth not correspond with the mystery of the feast. For if the fatherland hath its colors, then let the Church also have its colors, as is evident.

§. 220.

What is Necessary to Know Concerning the Ecclesiastical Language?

The language of divine services is twofold: $\alpha$) symbolic (*képnyelv* [symbolic language]), and $\beta$) verbal (*szóbeli nyelv* [spoken language]).[^332]
In order that a man may understand the symbolic language of divine services, he must first learn what essence or what symbolic meaning each individual service hath according to its theological sense, and therein each individual liturgical action.
As for the verbal language, concerning it we indicate this: that it must be, according to the common law of each church (*juxta jus commune*), more or less distinct from the common and ordinarily spoken tongue (*köznyelv* [vernacular]), and unchangeable, that is, a dead language (*holt nyelv* [dead language]).

(p. 285)

The difference between the Eastern and Western Churches — regarding the discipline of the ecclesiastical language — is this: that the Western Church, according to its particular law (*juxta jus privatum*), useth only one[^333] dead language in the Divine Liturgy, whereas the Eastern Church according to its particular law (*juxta jus privatum*) useth more.[^334]
That an ecclesiastical language properly established is not permitted to be altered or uprooted, and that in the Eastern Churches it must also be a dead language, is attested by Benedict XIV of blessed memory, who thus writeth: “Constantem, firmamque esse disciplinam, ne Missae idioma mutetur, et si mutet lingua vernacula; sed eo sermone Missa celebretur, quo celebrata est ab initio, etiam si illa lingua exoleverit apud vulgus, ejusque peritiam viri docti dumtaxat habeant.”[^335]
More especially doth Benedict XIV of blessed memory forbid that in the Eastern Churches an already consecrated and dead language should be cast out and uprooted on the ground that the people no longer understand it because they now speak another living language; for thus he writeth: “Sed non in eo controversiae cardo vertitur. Illud quaeritur, an si, quae vernacula fuit, vulgari alio sermone in populum invecto doctorum dumtaxat virorum facta fuerit lingua, divina Officia et Missa in novum vulgi sermonem convertenda sint. Id vero est, a quo semper abhorruit et abhorret Catholica Ecclesia.”[^336]

§. 221.

Brief Explanation Concerning the Paschalion.[^337]

According to the statute of the Ecumenical Council of Nicaea (held in the year 325), the Feast of Pascha may occur on the days from March 22 unto April 25; for this Council decided “to celebrate the Pascha of Christ on the first Sunday following the full moon which occurreth either on the very day of the vernal equinox (March 21), or the first after it.” — If this full moon should occur on the very day of the vern- (p. 286)

al equinox (March 21) on a Saturday, then the Pascha of Christ may be celebrated on the following Sunday (March 22), for the Jews will celebrate their Passover on Saturday. — But if this full moon should occur on the very day of the vernal equinox on a Sunday, then, in order not to celebrate Pascha together with the Jews on that same Sunday, the Pascha of Christ is postponed unto the following Sunday. — The latest possible day of the Pascha of Christ is the 25th day of April, because the March full moon may occur before the equinox, which according to our Julian Menologion is March 20; on this account one must await the full moon after March 21 after the passage of 28 days; yet even then, if this 28th day should occur on a Sunday, then on account of the Jewish Passover the Christian Pascha is to be celebrated on the following Sunday after the passage of 7 days, and thus after the passage of 35 days, that is, the 25th of April.
These 35 paschal days — between March 22 and April 25 — are designated with the letters of the Cyrillic alphabet with the addition of the letter “Ѫ”[^338] and they are called the “paschal letters” or “boundary letters.” By means of these 35 letters the Paschalion is composed.[^339] And therefore if we wish to know when in any given year the Pascha of Christ and the movable seasons fall, we must first know what the “boundary key,” that is, the “paschal letter” is in that year; and knowing this letter, everything that we wish to know will be unlocked by this key, if only we look into the table. (See Table I.) For example: in the year 1891 the boundary key will be “ь”, therefore the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee will be February 10; Pascha: April 21; the Descent of the Holy Spirit: June 9.
Besides this table, there are also other tables in which without any labor we can find the boundary key, for in these it is already given. (See Table II.) For example: for the year 1891 the boundary key is “ь”, for the year 1893: “ж”.
